ShinyHunters says it breached the FBI and stole employee data
2026-09-23

ShinyHunters says it breached the FBI and stole employee data

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
The data breach is in retaliation for alleged disinformation published by the FBI about ShinyHunters’ cybercrime methods, according to the group. Cybercriminal group ShinyHunters said it breached the US Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) yesterday (22 September) and stole the personal information of a number of current and former FBI employees. A screenshot shared by the group with a number of outlets reportedly showed information ShinyHunters had obtained on 5,000 FBI agents, including names, addresses, phone numbers and details on the employees’ spouses. Reuters said it could not verify whether the screenshot was real, but was able to partially verify the authenticity of some of the information displayed – the outlet was able to match details from the leak to FBI employees in at least 10 instances, one of which was FBI director Kash Patel. However, the publication said it could not establish where the data came from, or whether it had been stolen from the FBI’s internal systems as claimed by ShinyHunters. According to 404 Media, which cited an FBI spokesperson, ShinyHunters was able to instigate the breach through a zero-day exploit in an Oracle product called PeopleSoft, after which the group managed to access AWS GovCloud servers and download data. The total exfiltrated data amounts to between 2TB and 3TB, according to the publication. In a statement first posted to the group’s website on the dark web, ShinyHunters said it had targeted the FBI due to a report the agency published about the group earlier this year, which detailed the group’s methods and advised people not to pay if targeted for ransom. ShinyHunters said the FBI made false allegations about the group and its cybercrime methods in the report – including alleged usage of threatening phone calls and false claims of compromising information – and has given the FBI one week to “correct or simply remove” the report. It’s unclear what the group plans to do with the data if the one-week deadline passes. When asked by BleepingComputer if it would release the stolen data if the FBI failed to correct or remove the report, ShinyHunters said: “No comment.” Commenting on the FBI breach, Closed Door Security CEO William Wright told SiliconRepublic.com: “Hell hath no fury like a ShinyHunter scorned. “This is a retaliation attack, which not only demonstrates that no organisation is safe from the group, but also the fact that they will go to any length to protect their image. “The group clearly wants to control the narrative around their activities, ensuring nothing is said that could dent their reputation. Just like any organisation, reputation is everything, even in the dark and unruly world of cybercrime.” Wright also said Oracle needs to act fast to mitigate the zero-day vulnerability that allegedly allowed this breach to occur. “It is essential Oracle takes steps to understand the [exploited] CVE [Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures] and whether it is already known to them. If not, an emergency update is essential,” he said. “Any gaps between this announcement and a patch being released will only leave organisations vulnerable. Other threats actors will use the gap to exploit the CVE and launch further attacks.” ShinyHunters has been linked to a number of high-profile breaches in recent months. In March, the prolific group claimed responsibility for a breach of the European Commission’s Europa.eu platform, in which a reported 350GB of data, across multiple databases, was accessed and stolen. In May, ShinyHunters claimed responsibility for hacking education management platform Canvas and issued a ransom to Canvas parent company Instructure. Some days later, Instructure reached an agreement with the group. Recently, ShinyHunters targeted rival cybercrime group Clop by hacking and defacing its ransomware leak site in an escalation of a dispute between the two groups. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. Related: data, crime, cyberattacks, US, cybersecurity, breaches, FBI Colin Ryan is a senior reporter/production editor at Silicon Republic

Xbox cutting 268 jobs amid widespread unit consolidation
2026-09-23

Xbox cutting 268 jobs amid widespread unit consolidation

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
As part of the consolidation, several Xbox studios will be consolidated under Call of Duty maker Activision. Microsoft’s gaming division Xbox has announced plans to layoff 268 employees, as the organisation enters a period of consolidation. The company intends to bring several of its studios under the banner of Activision, the publisher behind games such as Call of Duty, Crash Bandicoot and Spyro the Dragon. Studios Rare and Age of Empires-maker World’s Edge will be overseen by Activision. Activision was purchased by Microsoft in 2023 for $69bn and will also lead the development of the next Halo game, according to Matt Booty, Xbox’s chief content officer. The layoffs are expected to impact employees at Halo Studios, which will continue to support the Halo community and games in-market. Bethesda, which is responsible for popular game series Fallout and Elder Scrolls, will expand to include studio Obsidian Entertainment, which will continue to work on its current projects – which includes survival game Grounded and a new Fallout title. Video game developer King, which makes Candy Crush, will now absorb Microsoft Casual Games, while Playground and Turn 10 will merge as one studio to focus on the Forza and Fable franchises. In a statement regarding the layoffs, Booty said: “We are eliminating 268 roles across Halo Studios, other first-party studios and the XGS management and central functions layer. The actions completed since July, inclusive of the studio divestitures, bring us roughly three-quarters of the way through previously announced restructuring. “I am deeply grateful for what our colleagues have built, and I know how difficult today will be for those leaving and the teams around them. Second, we are reorganising our studios. The goal is to strengthen our franchises and games by operating fewer business units, aligning groups that already work closely together and focusing our publishing expertise.” Booty added: “Our focus should be on supporting those affected and treating one another with care and respect. I will spend time with our studios over the next week. At our 6 October town hall, we will come together as an All-Xbox team to reflect on our progress and look ahead to one of the strongest game lineups in our history.” In July, Microsoft announced that it was laying off 4,800 people, including 3,200 at Xbox, amid plans to cut costs and flatten its organisational structure in response to AI and a changing environment. Microsoft Patents Technique for Inserting Ads Into Videogames While You’re Playing Them
2026-09-23

Microsoft Patents Technique for Inserting Ads Into Videogames While You’re Playing Them

Futurism | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Trying to escape the increasingly dystopian real world with a jaunt into the virtual one? A new system patented by Microsoft might make that easier said than done. Recently, Microsoft published a brand-new patent application for an “ad credit” system meant to seamlessly pump paid promotional material into video games. That’s according to Respawn First, which noted that the system would rely on signals relayed by the game engine to choose opportune times to serve gamers in-game commercials. These would vary from game-to-game, but generally encompass pre-existing events like boss fights, loading screens, and transitions out of cutscenes. When the system receives a positive signal, it stops the game in its tracks to serve either a video ad or static image, which also resets a timer counting down until the system goes back on the hunt for another in-game signal. “In non-interactive audiovisual content such periodic breaks can be placed at a variety of predictable times in the content without adversely affecting the user experience,” the Microsoft patent declares. “However, interactive software applications which stream interactive audiovisual content can provide a more varied user experience and predicting when to suspend interaction and present promoted audiovisual content can be challenging.” Called “Contextually Aware Management of Interactive Software Application Access,” it isn’t known if Microsoft has begun rolling it out to games, or if it even plans to. Overall, it appears geared toward free-to-play games on the Xbox and PC, Respawn notes — though given the slow creep of publisher-driven mechanisms like microtransactions, it may be just a matter of time before the context-aware ad system makes its way to paid titles as well. Software Engineer Says AI Is Causing Chaos Among Coders Who Now Just “Press Enter” All Day, Have Stopped Thinking or Understanding Software
2026-09-23

Software Engineer Says AI Is Causing Chaos Among Coders Who Now Just “Press Enter” All Day, Have Stopped Thinking or Understanding Software

Futurism | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
The advent of powerful AI coding tools has quickly made programmers heavily reliant on the tech, with experts warning that they’re slowly but surely losing the ability to write code by hand. It’s a growing existential crisis that’s significantly raising the chance of errors or security issues in AI code that hasn’t been properly vetted. Experts are warning of a “class divide” between those, who are over relying on AI tools and more experienced coders, or “craftsmen,” who are left wading through the former’s group’s output and applying any necessary fixes. Meanwhile, coders who’ve been instructed to use nothing but AI in a race to the bottom are losing their minds, saying they’re relegated to pushing buttons instead of taking pride in their work and building meaningful products themselves. As a user who goes by “v0xium” argued on X, the situation is getting out of hand. In a post that’s been viewed over seven million times and liked tens of thousands of times, the user warned that “it is over.” “The state of engineering right now is horrible,” the user wrote. “It has been half a month since I started a new role at a big company. Nobody knows anything here.” The engineer explained that everything at the unnamed company is being made by Anthropic’s coding assistant Claude Code. And while everybody is “being forced to ship as much as they can,” and pushing code isn’t a “bottleneck,” operations are still “slow.” “People are working 12 to 13 hours a day just to press enter,” v0xium wrote. “Everyone, literally everyone, from an L1 to an L7 engineer here is doing the same thing. Talk to Claude.” That means there’s “no sense of victory” and “nobody is resolving bugs.” “In reality, nobody is thinking anymore,” the engineer concluded. “Everything is done by LLMs. It is so soul-sucking.” The concerning tweet thread drew a somber reaction. Even X owner Elon Musk chimed in with a “yikes.” “We are turning an entire generation of technical experts around the world into the equivalent of retirees pushing a button in front of casino slot machines,” podcast host and venture capitalist Chamath Palihapitiya responded. “Essentially the brain rot of doom scrolling social media is coming for your development team…” The comments highlight an alarming fundamental shift, with AI tools effectively stripping engineers of the cognitive tasks they once found fulfilling. That tedium could result in companies pushing uninspired and buggy products. “The value of automated software is not making the software faster,” Palihapitiya added. “It is understanding your intention and making the software BETTER.” A recently-coined term perfectly describes this behavior: “meat proxies,” or people who regurgitate the output of AI chatbots verbatim without ever critically engaging with it. “Most software engineers are facing an identity crisis bordering on depression,” VC firm Menlo Ventures partner Deedy Das tweeted earlier this year. “The craftsmen are tired. Day after day, their workload grows. Bugs seep into production. No one seems to care. Another round of AI is thrown at it. Their animosity to their colleagues rises.” “Eventually, they give up,” he added. “The craft they loved is dead.” More on AI coding: Software Engineers Are Facing an Existential Crisis As They Drown In Horrendous AI Code

Trump Says No Need to Regulate AI, Since That Strategy Is Working Fine for Climate Change

Trump Says No Need to Regulate AI, Since That Strategy Is Working Fine for Climate Change

Futurism | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
If you’ve even so much as glanced at the news recently, you may have picked up on the raging debate over AI regulation in the US. Stronger regulations are incredibly popular with Americans from across the political spectrum — but not so much with their head of state, Donald Trump. In his speech to the United Nations General Assembly in New York City today, Trump blasted those calling for any kind of pause or slowdown to AI development. His rationale is stunning: near-total deregulation is working out great for the environment, he suggested, so why not replicate that success in the realm of AI? “The United States… totally rejects any attempt to construct a globalist scheme to control for the artificial intelligence being spoken of so much now,” Trump rambled to the various heads of state and their delegations. “Yet the very same people who said we’ll all be dead in 12 years because of global warming — a name since reborn to ‘climate change,’ because the planet was cooling, not warming, and nobody was dead — these are the same people that are now saying that AI is going to kill us all, that robots are going to attack us, that everything is going to be a total disaster.” Trump also took the opportunity to introduce his new rebrand of the tech, from artificial intelligence to “super intelligence.” “Herein after, officially called, ‘super intelligence’ — changing the name — and that the use of the word ‘artificial’ makes intelligence fake, it makes it sound fake. It’s actually not fake, it’s actually amazing — but we have to be careful,” Trump maundered, seemingly in contradiction of his statement that there’s nothing to be worried about. “In other words, welcome to the new world of super intelligence.” There are a few things worth breaking down here. First and most obviously, climate change is actively killing people across the world, via extreme heat waves, glacial melt-off resulting in freak mudslides, and prolonged droughts resulting in devastating wild fires, to name just a few examples from this past summer. It’s probably no wonder the president doesn’t acknowledge these events: he’d have to see them first. As Trump is an incredibly rich guy in the wealthiest country on the planet, he’s unlikely to ever interact with the victims of our rapidly warming world, who rank among the poorest and most destitute around the globe. Trump’s comments also reveal his socioeconomic outlook in another way. The climate movement, which he insists has moved on to the next issue-of-the-week, AI, is still alive and well in grassroots organizations, international coalitions, and academic circles. What’s changed is the attitude of American corporations and billionaires, who have made a hard pivot away from green capitalism in order to embrace Trump’s “drill baby, drill” energy regime. In hand-waving concerns about climate change and AI as hysterical sloganeering, Trump is signaling to the UN his intention to ignore both issues — a declaration that puts the rest of the world on the hook for his country’s many, many messes. Former Air Traffic Controller Terrified New AI System Will Hallucinate and Cause Catastrophe
2026-09-22

Former Air Traffic Controller Terrified New AI System Will Hallucinate and Cause Catastrophe

Futurism | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
As the US federal government rolls ahead with its controversial plans to deploy an AI air traffic control system across some of the nation’s busiest airports, one former air traffic controller is speaking out about the unprecedented risks involved. In an appearance on News Nation, 13-year air traffic control veteran Todd Sheridan Yeary made it clear that the new system — called the Strategic Management of Airspace, Routes and Trajectories, or SMART — is little-understood, as the aviation community is still asking basic questions about how it’s actually going to be used. “The first that comes to mind is, what is the tool going to be used for?” Yeary asked. Whether SMART is used as a predictive tool or a prescriptive stand-in for human decisions remains an open question, and the answer is incredibly important for anybody worried about aviation safety. “If AI is going to be more prescriptive in terms of how you resolve these conflicts at airfields, particularly busy airports… that’s a different question, because air traffic control is a dynamic environment,” Yeary said. “You cannot just put data in and expect that that data is still going to be good, because the conditions of air traffic control are constantly changing. So I want to see how they want to use it, but I’m a little suspect, and very conservative about its utility right now.” Asked what his biggest concern was, Yeary told News Nation “one word: hallucination.” “AI has been known to produce data and information that is not accurate and not verfieid, and so what are going to be the sources from which AI is getting this information?” he worried. “If it’s from airline schedules that’s one thing… there has to be some room for there to be an override, and that override has to come from the controller on duty.” Discussing the planned roll-out — SMART’s “soft launch” is coming first to the extremely busy airfield around the DC metro area — Yeary didn’t mince words: “anything other than a simulator to roll this out is a problem.” “Until we really have a chance to really kind of test on real-life-like scenarios, it is very difficult to say we’re going to rely on this information in a real time exercisem” Yeary said. “I think there’s a lot more that we don’t know, and I don’t want to try to find out in the middle of a scenario that is going on in real-time at an airport.” The SMART rollout comes amid a major aviation communications failure on Monday, where a core telecoms line and its redundancy went down at the same time near Philadelphia. The ensuing chaos left hundreds of planes delayed for hours across the east coast, and raised important questions as to whether the country’s ailing air traffic control system — and its overworked flight control personnel — will be able to shoulder the burden of a highly experimental AI system. Tesla Instructs Firefighters to Call Two Separate Support Hotlines in the Event of a Robotaxi Accident
2026-09-22

Tesla Instructs Firefighters to Call Two Separate Support Hotlines in the Event of a Robotaxi Accident

Futurism | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Firefighters and emergency responders already consider robotaxis a pain in their backsides. And Tesla’s gilded Cybercab, free of steering wheels or pedals, isn’t going to make their lives any easier. According to new guidance posted by the Elon Musk owned automaker, a firefighter who has to deal with a Cybercab in an emergency or that had an accident might need to call not one, but two different Tesla support support hotlines and wait for an employee to assist them with their request — an awfully clunky procedure in a profession where time is of the essence. The process is even more painstaking if a firefighter has to move the vehicle themselves. Recall that Cybercabs have no steering wheels or pedals. To control one, you have to first have to call support to gain control of the vehicle, according to the guidance. Then emergency responders can get access to its virtual onboard controls, which are of course located on a touchscreen. Try using that while wearing heavy gloves. The unstated premise is that Tesla is basically expecting firefighters to intimately know these details and more about its vehicle ahead of an actual emergency — as if they needed any more on their plates. Tesla, thoughtfully, says it’s placed a QR code on the bottom left corner of Cybercab windshields in case emergency responders need to remember what to do. There’re heaps of other details firefighters should know ahead of time: which wires to cut to completely disable the vehicle if necessary, how to open its doors that don’t have handles on the outside, how to pull it over, how to cage it in and stop it from moving. Or on the safety side of things, how the car might still be in autonomous driving mode even if it’s sitting still and the autonomous mode indicator is off, or that trying to start the car in low voltage power — so you can use its central touchscreen, for example — might start a fire. This might be placing too much extra responsibility for first responders, former fire chief and the director of fire and emergency services at the University of Florida’s design college Jeffrey Lindsey told Fast Company. “When you arrive on the scene of an incident, your main focus in the very beginning is scene safety to make sure everything’s safe. You’re taking in the whole scene. You’re trying to figure out what’s going on,” he said. In many cases, it’s just one responder arriving at a scene, and calling a support center for help adds another task to an already “full plate of things they have to accomplish.” Waymo robotaxis have already shown how these self-driving vehicles can be a source of frustration for first responders. Earlier this year, San Francisco’s Department of Emergency Management complained that police and firefighters were being expected to act as glorified “roadside assistance” for immobilized Waymos that suffered malfunctions. The comments came during a hearing to discuss how the robotaxi company’s Bay Area fleet descended into chaos during a city-wide power outage last December, causing the confused robotaxis, with no traffic lights to guide them, to pile up in intersections and block traffic. The Cybercab represents the most novel form of this issue, compounded by the fact that it has no traditional driver controls. Perhaps, as one expert suggests, robotaxi manufacturers might need to develop some kind of industry-wide standard operating procedures for first responders. “The central issue, in my view, is the need for independent research to develop standardized, manufacturer-neutral emergency-response protocols,” Prabodh Panindre, a professor at New York University who studies technology and fire science, told FastCo. These protocols, he said, should ideally be developed alongside the emergency responders. Robin Williams’ Daughter Horrified by People Creating AI Conspiracy Videos of Her Dad Long After His Death

Almost a decade after his tragic passing, iconic comedian Robin Williams is being remembered in the worst way possible: by mind-numbing AI slop video tributes that gullible netizens are sharing with abandon on social media. His surviving daughter and film director Zelda Williams has been watching with dismay. “That supposedly ‘private video’ of dad that I’ve been shown talking about conspiracies is clearly AI, and not even particularly convincing AI,” she tweeted on Monday. “Anyone who claims to be a fan who believes it clearly listened to his movies on mute because that voice is robotic and terrible.” A brief search on the Elon Musk-run social media site reveals a pixelated 13-second clip in which an AI-generated Robin Williams claims to have “been in this industry for a long time,” adding that he’d “seen things.” It’s not immediately clear if it’s the video Zelda Williams was referring to, but given how widely it’s been making its rounds, there’s a good chance it is. “The real elite, the ones at the very top, they don’t just play power games, they worship something much darker,” the AI imitation of Williams added, conspiratorially. In 2023, she found AI models that were used to mimic his voice “personally disturbing.” Two years later, after text-to-video generators became popular, she sent out a similar plea. “Please, just stop sending me AI videos of Dad,” she wrote at the time. “Stop believing I wanna see it or that I’ll understand, I don’t and I won’t.” More on AI slop: How Three Brothers Built an AI Slop Empire by Buying Legitimate News Sites and Turning Them Into Zombie Content Farms That They Say Get 50 Million Page Views per Month

Spotify’s is giving you the keys to its recommendation algorithm with US launch of ‘Taste Profile’

TechCrunch | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Spotify is giving listeners a new tool to shape the recommendations it delivers to their app’s Home feed. The music streaming giant on Wednesday began rolling out a feature called “Taste Profile” to its Premium subscribers in the U.S. that allows users to see, for the first time, how Spotify understands their taste across music and other audio. Users can request changes using natural language commands to fine-tune their recommendations, or make broader changes. Powered by Spotify’s AI, Taste Profile was first unveiled in March at the SXSW conference in Austin by Spotify co-CEO Gustav Söderström. However, the feature was initially available in beta only to listeners based in New Zealand. Now, the feature is arriving in Spotify’s largest market in terms of traffic and revenue, where it will be put to a larger test among listeners ages 18 and up. In some ways, Taste Profile essentially gives you the keys to Spotify’s algorithm. It is how the streaming platform chooses what music and podcasts to suggest, including in its flagship Discover Weekly playlist, its Made for You recommendations, and the highly anticipated annual review known as Spotify Wrapped. Though Spotify’s algorithmic understanding of a listener’s habits is largely credited as the secret to its success, those recommendations don’t always reflect users’ evolving tastes. The platform often ends up recommending too-similar music when a user would rather branch out. Now, by editing their Taste Profile directly, Spotify users can request more variety or ask for more or less of a certain genre, artist, or overall vibe. The feature could be used to remove recommendations based on music that users may regularly stream, but not necessarily care to see more of — for instance, sleep sounds played at night, or whatever music a teenager plays while in the car on their parent’s account. Until now, Spotify has offered some options to remove music from your Taste Profile, but they only let you exclude certain tracks or playlists. More recently, the company added a much-requested toggle switch that would prevent kids’ music from impacting their parents’ recommendations. To access Taste Profiles on the mobile app, first tap on your profile pic, then scroll down and select Taste Profile. From here, you’ll be able to explore how Spotify currently understands your taste across music, podcasts, and audiobooks. Under “Tell us more,” you’ll then enter what you’d like to adjust and send the message. After a few hours, your app’s Home Page will reflect a different set of suggestions. Spotify says the feature can be used when you want to explore a new genre or era, but aren’t sure where to start, or if you want to adjust the vibe of the music — like if you want more energizing tracks. You can also tell Spotify what sort of podcasts and audiobook you want to better shape those recommendations. The feature is still in beta. The company didn’t offer a timeline as to when it would reach a wider audience. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. LinkedIn adds new tools to fight fake profiles and bogus work histories
2026-09-23

LinkedIn adds new tools to fight fake profiles and bogus work histories

TechCrunch | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. AI has made it significantly easier for people to create fake accounts and identities to engage in online manipulation and scams. On Wednesday, LinkedIn announced a new set of tools to combat the problem while helping people and companies better control their presence on the platform. The company says it will now let members vouch for each other’s experience and give businesses new controls to manage who associates with their brand, among other changes. The new features arrive at a time when impersonation has become an internet-wide problem, not one limited to LinkedIn. However, on a network where your professional persona includes your work experience, past job titles, certifications, university degrees, and more, being able to verify you are who you say you are is even more critical. Such claims can lead to career opportunities and advancement, prospects that those fudging or lying on their resumes haven’t earned. “The backdrop for all this is that faking credibility has never been cheaper or easier than it is today; and conversely, showcasing credibility has never mattered more,” LinkedIn VP of Product, Oscar Rodriguez, told TechCrunch in an interview. To date, LinkedIn’s existing verification tools have been used to verify 115 million users and over 700,000 companies on the platform, he noted. “We’ve been invested in [verification] because we believe that authenticity will be the single most valuable currency on the internet,” Rodriguez added. For starters, LinkedIn will now allow members to vouch for their colleagues and classmates, past or present. This feature is not the same as an endorsement, where you can recommend the member to others, but is more of a way to vouch that someone has the experience they claim on their profile. In short, it’s a way of saying, “Yes, I worked with this person during this time,” not whether you enjoyed it. The addition includes a number of built-in protections against abuse. For instance, the person doing the vouching must already have a verified profile, and they must have been connected to the person for at least a year. They also have to have a secure account that uses two-factor authentication to decrease the risk of hackers gaining access to their account to perform these vouches. Another feature, which had previously been piloted with recruiters and execs, will allow companies to remove accounts that falsely claim employment from appearing on their page and in the search results associated with their page. To use this feature, page admins will be able to visit the account that belongs to the person falsely claiming employment at the company, then manually remove them. LinkedIn is also testing a setting that will allow page admins to require that any member account going forward that tries to associate with the page complete some sort of workplace verification, like a verification done through their work email address. When pages remove the member profile from their page, it won’t change the member’s LinkedIn profile — that’s something members themselves will retain control over. However, there will no longer be a clickable link to that company’s page from their profile, and their work experience won’t display as verified. “We hope that this provides signals and context to members to make decisions,” Rodriguez said. He also clarified this feature focuses on full-time employees, not contractors or field workers, though they will have other means of claiming their rightful association with the company that employs them. Plus, LinkedIn will expand its partnerships with more companies to allow members to showcase their verified identities beyond LinkedIn. On this front, Truecaller and PeerSpot will join existing partners like Adobe and UserTesting in this effort. For instance, Truecaller members can add their LinkedIn verified identity so it displays to the recipient when they place a call. Overall, the new features expand LinkedIn’s push to invest in verification, something that it believes benefits members directly. “We have seen how, for members that are verified, they derive a lot of benefits from all of this,” said Rodriguez. “They receive, on average, 50% more post views than non-verified profiles, and they get 90% more impressions as well,” he noted. PitPro’s first tire-changing robot goes live in Canada
2026-09-23

PitPro’s first tire-changing robot goes live in Canada

TechCrunch | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. If you live in Canada, this year’s set of snow tires might be installed by a robot. San Francisco startup PitPro Automation said Wednesday that it has installed its first automatic tire-changing system at a tire shop in Calgary, Alberta. The startup says its robotic system, first revealed last year, can swap a full set of four tires in under 15 minutes. The first deployment is at a Kal Tire location — one of Canada’s largest independent tire service chains. PitPro is one of myriad startups trying to disrupt different parts of the car ownership experience. While founder and CEO Jeremy Conrad told TechCrunch he ultimately wants to move into other kinds of automated maintenance, he thinks there’s big opportunity to build a business around tires in the near term. Changing tires is “backbreaking” work, and tire shops are facing a major labor shortage, he said in an interview. Even when shops can find people for the job, he said, those workers often leave within a few months to a year. “One shop manager was telling me they had to get special permission to pay more because a Panda Express opened across the street and it took about half their employees,” he said. PitPro’s system is designed to slide right into existing maintenance bays. It features two boxy robots, each roughly the size of an oven, that use a mix of sensors and a robotic arm to locate and loosen the lug nuts. The robots store the lug nuts, pull off each wheel, and replace it with a new or rotated tire. PitPro has, to date, mostly talked up plans to deploy its tire-changing robots with commercial fleets. It’s still focused on that line of business, Conrad said, and the startup has former Ford Pro executive Ted Cannis as an adviser. (“He’s got relationships with basically kind of every major fleet in the United States, and he’s you know been very helpful in making introductions for us on that,” Conrad said.) But Conrad believes tire chains are a major opportunity as well, not just because of the labor shortage, but because of the constant demand for tire changes — especially in Canada, where switching to and from winter tires is a yearly occurrence for most car owners. Conrad said PitPro is focused on chains, rather than smaller shops, since they tend to have standardized layouts and offer greater potential for wider deployment down the road. On top of that, mechanics can still use the bay that PitPro’s tech occupies for other maintenance, increasing the chance that shops will be willing to try it out, Conrad said. The shift to real-world deployment has had its challenges, according to Conrad. Tire shops tend to operate with the bay doors open, which exposes PitPro’s robots to all kinds of dust, debris, and weather. The startup had to account for all that, not only to keep the system robust, but also to make sure the sensors maintain an accurate view of the tires. Not everything is a challenge, though. During the interview, Conrad shared a photo from the Calgary installation showing where the robots had been almost completely covered in an unexpected substance: dandelion seeds. “We looked into it, and it’s not going to be a problem for us,” Conrad laughed. “But it’s just like… I had never thought about that we might need to deal with a dust cloud of dandelions.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Comma’s hands-off driving tech under investigation after 2 fatal crashes
2026-09-23

Comma’s hands-off driving tech under investigation after 2 fatal crashes

TechCrunch | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Comma.ai, the startup founded by hacker George Hotz, is facing a federal investigation after five reported crashes involving the company’s aftermarket hands-off driver assistance tech, two of which resulted in three deaths. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration’s Office of Defects Investigation (ODI) said this week that the reported crashes involved Comma.ai devices failing to detect or respond to slow or stopped vehicles in the same road lane. Across four of the crashes, as many as 11 people were injured, some seriously. Some of the crashes, including at least one of the fatal incidents, may have involved the use of “forked” or modified versions of Comma.ai’s software, according to the ODI. Comma.ai could not immediately be reached for comment. Hotz, who distanced himself from the startup’s day-to-day operations in 2022, did not immediately return a request for comment. Founded in 2015, Comma.ai makes devices that can be installed on a range of modern cars. The devices — and Comma.ai’s software, “openpilot” — can control a car’s Adaptive Cruise Control (ACC) and Automated Lane Centering (ALC) systems to perform functions “similar to Tesla Autopilot and GM Super Cruise,” according to the startup’s website. The goal is to “make driving chill.” Comma.ai’s website claims that “openpilot can steer, accelerate, and brake automatically for other vehicles within its lane.” Still, much like Tesla and GM, Comma.ai warns drivers that they “must always be capable to immediately retake manual control of the vehicle, by stepping on either pedal or by pressing the cancel button.” The company’s device uses a camera-based driver-monitoring system to watch for distracted or sleepy drivers. One of the two fatal crashes appears to have happened in February 2026 in Ascension Parish, Louisiana. A 2022 Toyota RAV4, which was running FrogPilot, a third-party fork of openpilot, struck a stopped first responder vehicle. Louisiana State Police released details of a crash in February that appear to match the report in the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration’s database. In that crash, the Toyota struck a “fully marked Gonzales Police Department unit, was unoccupied and positioned in the left lane with emergency lights activated while officers investigated a separate crash.” The state police said two rear-seat passengers of the Toyota died. The other fatal crash couldn’t be immediately identified by the report numbers cited by the ODI. Advanced driver assistance systems have historically struggled with stopped or slow vehicles, or other stationary objects. Tesla and Ford have faced similar investigations after their systems were involved in multiple crashes. Comma.ai’s own website states that the “performance” of openpilot Adaptive Cruise Control can be affected by “vehicles in the same lane that are not moving.” “When abrupt braking maneuvers are required,” the company writes, “openpilot is designed to be limited in the amount of deceleration and acceleration that it can produce.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Ema raises $77M as AI starts eating into enterprise software and services
2026-09-23

Ema raises $77M as AI starts eating into enterprise software and services

TechCrunch | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Ema, a startup that uses teams of AI agents to automate corporate processes across HR, IT, and finance, has raised $77 million in a new funding round as it aims to take on more of the work traditionally handled by enterprise software and IT services. The Series B round was led by Bengaluru-based venture firm Creaegis, with existing investors Accel, Section 32, and Prosus increasing their stakes. The financing brings the startup’s total funding to $140 million and more than quadruples its valuation from its last funding round in 2024. (Ema declined to disclose its latest valuation.) The round consisted entirely of primary equity, with no debt or secondary transactions, the startup confirmed to TechCrunch. The funding comes as AI is beginning to compete for dollars that businesses have traditionally spent on enterprise software and IT services. Startups, major AI labs, and established software companies are now fighting to capture that spending. Founded in 2023 by former Google and Coinbase executive Surojit Chatterjee and ex-Okta executive Souvik Sen, Ema is looking to expand its position in that market. The startup deploys its technology, which it calls “AI employees” — systems that coordinate multiple AI agents. These help carry out multi-step business processes across a company’s existing applications, rather than handling a single task at a time. Chatterjee sees that model eventually reducing companies’ reliance on traditional software products, including those sold as software-as-a-service (SaaS). Ema first “wraps” around an enterprise’s existing applications, he said, before customers can reduce their dependence on some of those products — and, in some cases, replace them altogether. “Many of our customers are already on the way to replace [large SaaS applications] completely, removing dependency on them, because they are mostly becoming like a database,” Chatterjee said. In recent months, major AI companies have also pushed deeper into the enterprise market where Ema operates. Anthropic has expanded efforts to bring Claude into companies’ core operations, including financial and legal work. Similarly, OpenAI has established teams of forward-deployed engineers who work alongside customers to put AI into production. Chatterjee, however, does not see the frontier AI labs as direct competitors. He told TechCrunch that Ema’s software can draw on more than 150 models, including frontier and open source models, while the startup focuses on the domain knowledge, integrations, and orchestration needed to automate business processes end-to-end. “Progress in frontier models is actually very beneficial to us,” Chatterjee stated. Ema’s approach is already gaining traction. The startup has more than 50 active enterprise deals, as well as over 1 million active enterprise users, and has handled more than 5 million actions and queries. Its customers include NTT DATA, Hitachi, ADP, PwC, Google, KPMG, Wipro, and Microsoft. Over the past two years, Ema said its revenue has grown 50-fold, while revenue bookings have surpassed $150 million. Chatterjee said the bookings figure includes the total value of multiyear contracts, including two- and three-year deals, rather than representing annual recurring revenue. He, however, declined to disclose the startup’s current annualized revenue run rate. Chatterjee told TechCrunch that more than 90% of Ema’s customers have expanded beyond their initial use case, with some deploying the technology across dozens of workflows. The startup’s net dollar retention rate is around 180%, he said, meaning existing customers are spending substantially more with Ema over time. Ema is also looking beyond the software itself. AI, Chatterjee said, can take over some of the implementation, integration, and consulting work that companies have traditionally paid IT services firms to perform around enterprise software. “A lot of the services companies are working with us,” Chatterjee said. “They are also dramatically changing or disrupting their own business models because they understand the human-forward model may not be the best model going forward.” Despite taking on work traditionally handled by software and services providers, Chatterjee said Ema has maintained gross margins of close to 80%. The startup, he noted, requires less human support as its AI systems learn from deployments, helping improve margins over time. Ema also does not charge customers based on software seats or the number of AI tokens they consume. Instead, Chatterjee said, its pricing is tied to the completion of tasks and business outcomes. Much of Ema’s new capital will go toward expanding its go-to-market operations, particularly sales and marketing, after spending its first years largely building the product, Chatterjee said. The Mountain View-headquartered startup has grown to nearly 200 employees and has its offices in Bengaluru, London, and Vancouver. Ema has so far focused primarily on customers in the U.S. and Europe. However, it now plans to expand into new markets over the next year, particularly across Asia-Pacific, South America, and parts of the Middle East. ‘We’re already fighting yesterday’s battle’: Greece’s prime minister gets candid about AI
2026-09-23

‘We’re already fighting yesterday’s battle’: Greece’s prime minister gets candid about AI

TechCrunch | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Greek Prime Minister Kyriakos Mitsotakis came to San Francisco on Monday night to promote Greece, but he also did something that heads of state on trade missions don’t typically do. In front of roughly 250 founders, investors, and operators, he said openly that he doesn’t have answers to many of the AI questions leaders around the world are more privately debating. The prime minister, speaking with this editor at an event hosted by Endeavor Greece, the local arm of a global nonprofit that supports entrepreneurs in emerging and growth markets, described his Bay Area trip as partly a fact-finding mission. He’d spent the morning touring Tesla and Sequoia Capital, among other stops, with plans to head this week to the U.N. General Assembly in New York. But the trip was also meant to build bridges; indeed, he was pitching anyone in tech who might consider setting up shop in Greece, which is set to regain developed market status next year from MSCI, the influential index provider. “I think it’s another indication that the economy is doing well and that Greece is no longer treated as a special case,” Mitsotakis said. Mitsotakis, who earned a master’s degree at Stanford and called the visit “a homecoming,” made the economic case first and foremost during our sit-down. Greece is paying down its debt at a record pace, and, he added, borrows more cheaply than the United States. “I don’t know if I should say this,” he told the crowd, smiling. It’s not an apples-to-apples comparison. Interest rates are lower across the eurozone, which accounts for part of the difference. But it makes for a strong talking point. As of this writing, Greece’s 10-year bond yield sits around 4.3%, versus roughly 5% for U.S. Treasuries. That would have been completely unimaginable during the debt crisis, when Greek yields topped 40% in 2012. Mitsotakis was just as eager to talk about how Greece has spent its money. For example, he said that much of the roughly €36 billion Greece received from the EU’s post-COVID recovery fund went into digital infrastructure, including an online portal that lets Greeks handle government paperwork without waiting in line, and a new supercomputer assembled with the help of Hewlett Packard Enterprise in the port town of Lavrio that he said will come online within months to power AI and scientific research in Greece. Unsurprisingly, he also listed policy changes that can matter even more to tech companies. Greece has changed how stock options are taxed, he said, loosened labor laws, and offers returning Greeks far lower taxes for up to seven years. We also talked about the country’s growing array of visa programs, though when asked how he measures the success of these, Mitsotakis said the government “still [has] work to do” on processing speed. He separately volunteered that there has been a stark turnaround at Greece’s public universities, which he said long embraced a “radical leftist approach,” looking down on corporations, and now spin out startups. Mitsotakis’s larger goal is to win back the talent Greece lost during its debt crisis, when many of the country’s younger citizens left because they had no other options. He also sees an opening: with American work visas getting harder to obtain, he suggested, more founders should consider hiring in Greece. It did strike me that the conversation took on a different tone when it turned to AI’s effects on society. Mitsotakis was refreshingly candid about not having a finished plan. Greece, for example, is instituting a ban on social media for children under 15 as of this coming January, a step many countries are similarly weighing or adopting. He suggested the ban may already be behind the times, however, considering the seemingly addictive nature of AI chatbots. “Sometimes I feel that we’re already fighting yesterday’s battle,” he said. “What does it mean for our kids to grow up with digital companions or boyfriends or girlfriends?” He was similarly cautious about AI in classrooms. Greece has run an education pilot with OpenAI aimed at reducing teachers’ administrative work, and he sees promise in personalized AI tutors. But he said the benefits depend on AI not replacing “the hard work of learning the basic skills.” He warned that students are already using chatbots to do their homework, adding: “complacency is a human trait.” When I asked him about AI infrastructure — Greece has been courting data center investment — he described the country as an outlier. Despite growing opposition to data centers across the globe because of the amounts of electricity and water required to power them, Mitsotakis said that “we have not had any significant reaction” in Greece. He pointed to the fact that Microsoft is building a cluster of data centers near Athens and that AWS recently announced a deal with Greece’s largest utility to build the country’s biggest data center in a former coal region. There, he said, the projects are welcomed. Unlike many politicians, he didn’t pretend to have answers about what comes next, either. Job displacement “is something which is going to happen,” he said, “and no government and no society is prepared for the speed with which it will happen.” As for the debate over whether AI development should slow down, he sided with the leaders of the frontier labs who’ve been calling for one. “If the people who are building the models are telling us, ‘We’re not really sure exactly what’s happening with these models,’” especially in how they improve themselves, he said, “we need to listen to them.” Some form of “smart regulation” is inevitable, he observed, and the U.S. will largely decide what it looks like. Still, he added, Greece would happily host the conversation. Humanity has created “a form of intelligence that is very quickly exceeding the capacity of the most advanced organ that evolution has ever created,” said Mitsotakis, raising the question of what it means to be human. Bringing together technologists with “the social scientists, the philosophers, the historians,” he said, “I cannot think of a better place to really have these discussions.” He has a point. Athens is where Socrates badgered his fellow citizens in the agora, the city’s ancient public square, about what makes a good life and a just society. Maybe the questions Silicon Valley is still debating will end up being settled there, too? When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Why California Just Declared a State of Emergency Due to El Niño

Why California Just Declared a State of Emergency Due to El Niño

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A Pacific Ocean wave washes up near a worker using heavy machinery to protect damaged beachfront homes with boulders on Sept. 14, 2026 in Dana Point, Calif. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings California’s governor Gavin Newsom has declared a state of emergency ahead of a potentially historic El Niño. In a statement released Sept. 22, Newsom said the declaration is meant to ensure communities have the resources they need to handle the impacts expected this winter. “California is strongest when we look out for one another. That is what this action is about,” Newsom said. “Giving communities the support they need, giving first responders the tools to do their jobs, and giving families the confidence that their state is ready.” Read more: How Should You Prepare For A Super El Niño? The state of emergency allows the government to mobilize state resources: readying flood-fighting supplies such as sandbags and pumps, preparing the National Guard to assist with flood response and search and rescue efforts, and fortifying roads and infrastructure. State environmental and natural resources agencies will also fast-track permitting and engage in other efforts to better prepare for flooding and landslides. El Niño typically brings wetter, more intense winters to California—which could leave the state more vulnerable to severe winter storms, heavy rainfall, landslides, and flooding. An El Niño year does not guarantee that these conditions will occur, but it does increase the likelihood. Climate change is also supercharging weather events, and El Niño is no exception. One study published in August found that El Niño events are now nearly 40% stronger than before the Industrial era. This year’s El Niño is predicted to be the strongest in recorded history. The governor’s office noted that the state has already experienced coastal flooding attributable to El Niño. The state can expect to see Kelvin waves, slow moving pulses of water that can stretch thousands of miles and raise sea levels along the coastline—increasing the risk for coastal flooding and erosion, and offshore Pacific hurricanes. Climate scientists also warn that the Pacific can expect to see a temporary 6-inch to 2-ft. rise in sea levels this winter. A number of southern California towns, including Malibu, Laguna Beach, and Long Beach, have already seen significant coastal erosion due to El Niño and Pacific hurricanes this year. The state has urged residents to take action to prepare for El Niño by signing up for emergency alerts and learning their flood risk, and having an evacuation plan and emergency kit at the ready. It’s all part of an effort to ensure that the state is not caught off guard by El Niño. “We are not waiting for this season’s storms to be on our doorstep,” Cal OES director Caroline Thomas Jacobs said in a statement. “By planning early and coordinating resources ahead of El Niño, we are working to ensure communities and first responders have the personnel, equipment, and support they need to prepare for severe weather, respond quickly when conditions change, and protect lives and property across California.”

America Must Win Back Its Global Standing

America Must Win Back Its Global Standing

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Sunrise in New York City on April 18, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings “From this day forward, a new vision will govern our land,” President Donald Trump declared in his inaugural address in January 2017. “From this moment on, it’s going to be America First.” An insular, populist nationalism, coupled with an antipathy toward globalist ideas and institutions, became the central theme and the clarion call of both his terms in the White House. President Trump has spent considerable energy lambasting the United Nations, an institution the United States helped create 80 years ago and has shaped ever since. He has derided it as an “unelected, unaccountable, global bureaucracy,” and little more than “a talking shop.” A majority of Americans no longer agree with their president. In a Sept. 2025 global poll commissioned by The Rockefeller Foundation,  61% of American respondents agreed that their country should cooperate with others to solve global challenges, even if doing so meant compromising on some national interests. Only 16% responded with a no. Such American support for international cooperation has only increased. When the same question was posed in the 2026 iteration of the poll, which surveyed more than 35,000 people across 34 countries, support in the U.S. rose by four percentage points to 65%, while the opposition fell by two percentage points to 14%. American public support for multilateral cooperation in 2026 exceeds that of Western Europe, where it stands at 61%. Despite President Trump’s attacks on the UN, 62% of Americans say they trust the institution—eight percentage points more than last year—while only 30% claim to distrust it. Nearly half of American respondents, 46%, say they comfortable with the UN playing a leading role on the world stage. Only 21% say they are uncomfortable. As global stability has eroded and peace has grown increasingly fragile, the desire for cooperation across border has increased among Americans. In the same poll, respondents were asked to choose three priorities from a list of 13 global issues: among American respondents, 40% ranked preventing wars and conflicts among their most pressing concerns. The message to President Trump is clear. For most of the last 80 years, America has acted multilaterally, even during its unipolar moment. Americans do not want the U.S. to act unilaterally in an increasingly multipolar era. They want their country to practice the politics of consensus building rather than coercion. I joined an initiative involving 100 former prime ministers and presidents from every continent that calls upon today’s leaders to build a stronger basis for cooperation in an increasingly fragmented and multipolar world. On Monday, on the sidelines of the United Nations General Assembly in New York, many of us came together to endorse core principles that could provide the basis for a new global consensus on solving shared problems and helping those in need. As the poll commissioned by the Rockefeller Foundation found, more than half of American respondents, 53%, would support their president publicly endorsing these principles of cooperation. This finding suggests that America is not a nation committed to an “America first and America only” ideology, but a nation with a strong sense of the enduring values that bind it to the wider world. Yet as American support for cooperation with the rest of the world rises, the inclination across the rest of the world to cooperate with America hangs in the balance. For decades, people everywhere have looked to America for hope and inspiration. That faith is now being  questioned. America’s appeal as global leader, the same poll found, has diminished. Only 39% of the respondents worldwide are comfortable with the U.S. playing a leading role on the world stage. And 34% of the respondents across the globe regard the U.S. as a major threat to their own countries. On that grim metric, Russia scored even higher, at higher 42%. China, surprisingly, fared better at 30%. When respondents to the 2026 poll were asked which countries they would be comfortable seeing take a leading role in world affairs, America ranked in the bottom half of the table. Canada outpolled America, with 60% of the respondents globally comfortable with it playing such a role, the highest vote recorded for any country. Several middle powers were viewed more favorably by respondents as potential global leaders: Japan (58%), Australia (55%), the United Kingdom (54%), Germany, Spain, and Italy (all 53%), France (51%) and South Korea (42%). While none of these countries comes remotely close to matching American economic and military power, America’s soft power has certainly suffered in recent years. It is estimated that 900 million people want to live somewhere other than their own country. Surveys asking people which country they would prefer to live in have consistently put America in the lead. According to Gallup data, 24% of respondents in 2009 named America as their preferred new home. By 2025, that preference had shrunk to a mere 15%. America can still regain its soft power. Americans have not lost their optimism or their sense of belief in a better future. In the 2026 poll, 55% of American respondents professed that, during a crisis, their own community would pull together. An even more decisive majority, 64%, believe that, as Americans they share a responsibility to help others, even in countries they don’t know. And 76% of American respondents believe it is possible to build a better future for the next generation. Americans need to convey these convictions more clearly and widely. Under pressure from an affordability crisis at home and military overreach abroad, Americans may not any longer be prepared to “bear any burden or pay any price.” But the values that underpin America are still embraced by a majority of its people. This suggests that America still has what it takes to resume the global leadership that is needed more than ever in our turbulent world.

What a Diesel Export Ban Would Mean for U.S. Consumers
2026-09-23

What a Diesel Export Ban Would Mean for U.S. Consumers

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A diesel fuel nozzle at a Chevron gas station in San Jose, Calif. Sept. 10, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings As fuel prices at the pump reach record highs since the outbreak of the war in Iran, Washington is warming to the idea of a temporary diesel export ban as a way to lower costs. “I’ve said let's not send out the diesel—we make a lot of diesel,” President Donald Trump said as he met with Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky in New York on Tuesday. “That could have a little bit of an effect on regular automobile gasoline because when you do that, you know, it’s a sort of a flow.” Trump echoed what a cacophony of Republican lawmakers, many representing farm states, have called for in the past few weeks. Senator Chuck Grassley of Iowa posted on social media Saturday: “High diesel prices ARE KILLING FARMERS INCOME.” Senator Dan Sullivan of Alaska, meanwhile, said Tuesday that the cost of diesel “is just too damn high” and an export moratorium should be in place until the conflict in Iran ends. Diesel recorded its highest average price on Tuesday amid the energy dearth caused by the war, averaging about $6.53 per gallon, according to the American Automobile Association. It is at least 90¢ higher than last month’s average, and a huge leap from the $3.75 mean just before the war. Ballooning prices threaten to impact the global economy as diesel powers most heavy-duty farming and industrial machinery as well as transport like trucks and trains. Higher diesel costs also add to the cost of moving goods and could worsen inflation, which the Federal Reserve has tried to tamp down. Trump referred to Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who said that the Administration is examining whether a diesel export ban is “feasible in terms of the overall refining capacity and whether a full or partial ban would work.” It’s unclear if, or when, the Administration will decide on a moratorium on diesel exports. Trump’s own Energy Secretary Chris Wright showed more skepticism: speaking to CBS News earlier this month, Wright said the U.S. is considering “all options of how you can move prices that are favorable for American consumers,” but he also argued that to address a shortage, “you want to keep as much energy flowing as possible.” Rising costs present a problem for an increasingly unpopular Trump heading into the November midterms, where the Republican Party he leads has to defend its slim majority in Congress. Disgruntled voters, according to some polls, believe the U.S. economy has suffered under Trump’s policies, with many from the GOP also souring on them. The U.S. is one of the world's largest diesel producers and exporters. Data from the U.S. Energy Information Administration show that American refineries produce about 5.3 million barrels of distillate fuel oils—referring to fuels that include diesel and home heating oil—per day. Diesel exports amount to about 1.5 million barrels daily, almost a fifth of the roughly 8 million barrels traded by sea each day. But since diesel is traded globally, prices in the U.S. are shaped by this balance of supply and demand. The recent price surge has been linked to production and shipping disruptions from the wars in Ukraine and Iran. Many Republican lawmakers pushing for the diesel export ban argue that it could provide American consumers with some relief. Last week, Rep. Tim Burchett of Tennessee filed bills to address rising diesel costs through export controls and argued that such controls “drive down prices while also helping Americans keep more money in their pockets.” Senate Majority Leader John Thune of South Dakota also indicated that he was “open to exploring” a diesel export ban to lower prices. Analysts from the Washington think tank Atlantic Council wrote in a Tuesday note that such an export ban “would likely create more problems than it would solve.” While the ban is expected to “almost certainly” push prices down for consumers in the Gulf Coast and the Midwest, it may pull prices up for those on the West Coast. “If the global price of diesel rises and supplies in the heartland cannot be routed to the coasts quickly enough, then these regions could see higher prices,” the analysts wrote. They also warned of knockon effects including a possible uptick in grocery prices. Garrett Golding, assistant vice president at the Federal Reserve Bank of Dallas, said in a social media thread on Monday that even with the swift dip in diesel prices, a diesel export ban would tighten the global balance enough to cause price increases that “will boomerang back” onto the East Coast—and to a smaller degree, the West Coast. Refineries will also have to reduce their run rate—the percentage of a refinery’s maximum capacity used to process crude oil into refined products, Golding added. This would lead to reduced production of gasoline, jet fuel, and other refined products, “which means higher prices.” The Atlantic Council analysts also warned that an export ban would have follow-on effects outside of the U.S., including pushing international diesel prices up, which could similarly “boomerang” on the country. The API opposes a ban and argues that doing so risks “making a difficult situation worse.” President and CEO Mike Sommers said in a statement, “We understand the administration is looking at every option to deliver relief, but restricting U.S. energy exports would only compound the problem—exacerbating refining challenges and ultimately hurting consumers.” The diesel export ban has been a point of contention for GOP lawmakers ahead of the November elections. Some from oil-producing states argue against the proposal on the basis that a ban could push prices down for producers under their jurisdiction. Outgoing Senator John Cornyn of Texas, the largest oil-producing state in the U.S., claimed that the ban is a “gimmick.” Cornyn’s colleague, Senator Lisa Murkowski of major oil producer Alaska, cast her doubts on the export ban to the Hill: “We’re talking about global supply, so I worry that we do something in the short-term … that doesn’t really move the needle.” Senator Mike Rounds of South Dakota also expressed criticism and said he is instead looking to other options, including restarting idled refineries. “The bigger problem we’ve got right now is, as I understand it, in California, we’ve already lost two more refineries because of California’s strict environmental rules,” Rounds told the Hill. “I would like to get those back up and operational again.” The reactions exhibit how keen Republicans are to appear that they are providing solutions to the rising costs of living. As Reuters’ energy columnist Ron Bousso put it, fuel costs are increasingly becoming a political liability, and for the wider GOP, “a politically popular ‘quick fix’ may prove difficult to resist.”

You Actually Don’t Need to See That Movie in IMAX
2026-09-23

You Actually Don’t Need to See That Movie in IMAX

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The TCL Chinese Theatre during opening weekend for "The Odyssey" in IMAX on July 18, 2026 in Hollywood, Calif. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings 2026 has, without a doubt, been the year of IMAX. That’s due in large part to Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey, which broke countless box office and IMAX records after its release in mid-July. But it’s not just The Odyssey. Films like Project Hail Mary and the year’s biggest blockbuster, Spider-Man: Brand New Day, played in the format, and IMAX theaters will be full through the end of the year, thanks to the final installment in the Dune franchise. In August, lines formed around the block at the AMC theater in New York City’s Upper West Side as people waited to secure coveted tickets to see Dune: Part Three in IMAX 70mm—even though the movie doesn’t come out until mid-December. But I would argue this fervor is misplaced. I would go so far as to say that IMAX is overrated. This trend didn’t come out of nowhere: IMAX has been on the rise in the past few years, as post-pandemic audiences are increasingly drawn to movie theaters for spectacle and eventization that can’t be replicated at home. In 2025, moviegoers flocked to see films like Sinners, One Battle After Another, F1, and the final Mission: Impossible in IMAX. The gold standard for cinephiles is the ultra-rare IMAX 70mm screening, which requires a projector large enough to play the giant 70mm film reels (unlike standard IMAX screenings, which use digital projection). Only 26 theaters in the United States can project 70mm IMAX, and the competition to get tickets to films like The Odyssey and Dune: Part Three at those theaters has created a resale market that rivals popular sporting events and concerts. IMAX’s popularity has been a boon for the box office and great for the movie industry, which has struggled to regain audiences after the COVID-19 pandemic. However, it’s also caused feelings of FOMO in those who aren’t able to secure tickets, or who don’t live near an IMAX theater (let alone the rare theater that can project IMAX 70mm). And many so-called IMAX theaters in the country are not actually true IMAX, meaning people paying extra for those screenings in “LieMAX” auditoriums aren’t even getting all the benefits of the format. This summer, social media sites were flooded with people sharing photos and videos of their Odyssey viewing experiences, looking for advice on how to secure a coveted ticket, or searching for one to buy off of someone else. There was a pervasive feeling of: If I’m not seeing The Odyssey in IMAX, am I really seeing it at all? While seeing a movie in IMAX is undoubtedly a great experience, it’s not necessary to enjoy a movie—or to be a true cinephile. At the end of the day, you’ll still get all of the intended narrative and emotional impact in a “regular” theater, even if you aren’t getting the full IMAX screen ratio. A 2014 study by psychologists Andreas M. Baranowski and Heiko Hecht compared sizes of screens with regard to viewer immersion and found that, while larger screens were more immersive, when participants were asked to evaluate the movie they had just seen, the screen size did not affect their answer. Filmmakers understand this as well. Though they might prefer you to see their movie in IMAX, they ultimately shoot with all formats and audiences in mind, knowing that not everyone will see it in IMAX. (Some people will even watch it on the backside of a plane seat!) When framing shots—even on an IMAX camera—they keep anything essential to the narrative in the core ratio that most people will see the movie in. Despite all the jokes about “watching a movie as Christopher Nolan intended,” in a recent interview with 60 Minutes, the director said he didn’t mind people watching his movies on iPads. “I’m very much in favor of and in awe of the easy access that we have now to films, for people to be able to immerse themselves in film and film history,” Nolan said in the interview, drawing a comparison to his childhood spent learning about film through VHS tapes. In the 2020s, movie projection technology is the best it’s ever been. If IMAX isn’t your jam, or if you can’t secure tickets, there are plenty of alternative ways to see movies that are better than all of human history has ever had access to. Dolby is a great option, with its enhanced color and immersive sound, and 35mm and 70mm screenings are plentiful among theaters around the country if you prefer to get the film experience. So, it doesn’t make you less of a cinephile if you can’t get to an IMAX screen (especially one that can show 70mm IMAX), or if you prefer to not view a movie in IMAX. For people with sensitivities to loud noises or bright lights, it may not be the best option. There is a difference, but it isn’t enough to stop you from enjoying the film. I saw The Odyssey twice: once in IMAX 70mm, and once in 70mm on a “regular” screen, and enjoyed the non-IMAX experience much better, as it felt less visually and auditorily overwhelming. The same sentiment can be found on various subreddits devoted to moviegoing. At the end of the day, no matter what theater you choose to view a film in, as long as you’re having fun and actively engaging with the work, you’re doing it right.
