The Next Four Years Will Decide the Ocean’s Future
2026-09-23

The Next Four Years Will Decide the Ocean’s Future

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Seaside scenery on La Digue Island, Seychelles on June 26, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings This summer, the ocean sent us another warning. In August, global sea-surface temperatures reached their highest level on record. Days later, the most comprehensive assessment of coral reefs ever published showed that increasingly frequent marine heatwaves are leaving reefs less time to recover between bleaching events. Global hard coral cover has declined by 9.5% compared with the 1980-2009 average, driven primarily by human-induced climate change. For me, this is deeply concerning, but also a reason to act. I come from Seychelles, an archipelago of 115 islands off the coast of East Africa where the ocean is inseparable from our lives, livelihoods, and future. It supports the food we eat, the jobs people depend on, and the resilience of our economy. During my years in office, I saw what is possible when governments make ocean protection a political priority. In 2020, during my presidency, Seychelles designated 30% of its waters for protection, years before the world adopted the global goal of protecting 30% of land and ocean by 2030. That experience taught me that ambitious conservation is possible when political commitment is matched by action. With fewer than four years remaining until 2030, the international community needs to bring that same determination to protecting the global ocean. Since governments adopted the global goal in 2022, countries have made important progress. Nearly 20% of land and inland waters are now designated as protected or conserved, and existing commitments could bring terrestrial protection to around 27% by 2030. But the picture at sea is very different. Around 10% of the global ocean is currently designated as protected or conserved, and existing commitments would raise that figure to only around 12% by 2030. The next phase of this global effort must therefore put much greater emphasis on the ocean. Governments need to establish more marine protected areas, strengthen national commitments where ambition remains too low, and move rapidly from targets to protection in the water. This matters far beyond conservation. Healthy marine ecosystems support fisheries and food security, sustain livelihoods and economies, store carbon, and help protect coastal communities from climate impacts. The recent coral findings make the consequences of delay clear. Marine heatwaves are becoming more frequent, leaving reefs less time to recover. Recovery is still possible, but ecosystems need time and the right conditions to recover. Climate action remains essential, but reducing pressures such as overfishing, pollution and damaging extraction can give vulnerable ecosystems a better chance of recovery. Well-designed and properly managed marine protected areas can give nature the space and time it needs. But drawing a boundary on a map is not enough. The 2026 Ocean Protection Gap Report finds that while around 10% of the global ocean is designated as protected or conserved, just 3.5% is assessed as effectively protected. A protected area cannot fulfill its purpose if damaging activities continue, management is weak, or long-term finance is missing. In Seychelles, we are confronting this challenge ourselves. Having designated more than 30% of our waters for protection, we are now undertaking the harder work of turning those protections into results in the water. Our Marine Spatial Plan Initiative is legally binding, including no-take areas where extractive activities are prohibited. But effective protection also requires management, monitoring, enforcement and long-term resources. The next four years, through the end of 2030, must therefore be about both expansion and effectiveness. Success should be judged by area on a map, and by whether biodiversity recovers and communities continue to benefit from healthy ecosystems. National action alone cannot close the ocean protection gap. The high seas account for roughly two-thirds of the global ocean, yet only a very small share is protected. Species migrate across borders, currents connect ecosystems, and damage in one part of the ocean can have consequences far beyond it. The High Seas Treaty changes what is possible. For the first time, governments have a global legal framework capable of enabling marine protected areas beyond national jurisdiction. Seychelles became the first African country to ratify the treaty because island states understand that protecting our own waters is not enough. Our future also depends on the ocean we share. Governments now need to turn this legal breakthrough into protection in the water by advancing an ambitious first generation of high seas marine protected areas, supported by strong science, effective governance, monitoring, and sufficient finance. Ambition must also be matched by resources. The new assessments estimate that around $16 billion is needed each year for marine protection under the 30% target, compared with only around $1.8 billion currently spent. Without the resources to manage them, protected areas risk remaining protected on paper rather than in practice. As leaders gather at the United Nations General Assembly in New York, ocean protection should be part of discussions about climate resilience, food security, and economic stability. COP17 next month in Armenia will soon provide another opportunity for governments to confront where progress is falling furthest behind. In 2019, I spoke from beneath the Indian Ocean because I wanted world leaders to understand that we could not leave the environmental problems we have created for another generation to solve. That warning is more urgent today. But there are also reasons for hope. Countries have shown that ambitious conservation is possible. This June, French Polynesia announced an expansion that will bring 1.4 million square kilometers—around 30% of its waters—under full protection from extractive industries once implemented. Samoa and Portugal have also made meaningful increases in effective protection over the past year. We now understand more about what effective protection requires. And with the High Seas Treaty in force, we have a powerful new means of safeguarding the ocean we all share. The years between now and 2030 must be a turning point for ocean protection. We need to protect more of the ocean, protect it well, and seize the opportunity of the High Seas Treaty. If we do, we will not only safeguard biodiversity, but strengthen food security, climate resilience, and livelihoods for generations to come. Matthew McConaughey and Woody Harrelson’s Disjointed But Endearing Brothers Coasts on Charm
2026-09-23

Matthew McConaughey and Woody Harrelson’s Disjointed But Endearing Brothers Coasts on Charm

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Matthew McConaughey, left, and Woody Harrelson in Brothers Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings As a nation still mourning Dolly Parton is all too aware, there aren’t many entertainers left who are embraced by Americans across demographics and ideologies. Among the few A-listers with bipartisan appeal are Matthew McConaughey and Woody Harrelson, separately but especially as longtime best friends and occasional co-workers—two sons of Texas who, as you’ve probably heard, might also be sons of the same biological father. So it makes sense that Brothers, an Apple TV comedy that casts them as exaggerated versions of themselves, seems built to satisfy as broad a range of their fans as possible. Disjointed but breezy, it coasts on the chemistry between its leads. Premiering with two episodes on Sept. 23, Brothers bills itself as being “based on a true rumor” whose plausibility Harrelson acknowledged in 2023: that McConaughey is the product of a tryst between his mom, Kay McConaughey, and Harrelson’s dad, the notorious contract killer Charles Voyde Harrelson, during her second divorce from James McConaughey. (Genetics aside, both families evidently have a flair for off-screen drama.) That’s certainly a big part of the show, supplying a central mystery and fueling tension between the stars after Kay, a.k.a. Ma Mac (Holland Taylor, playing delightfully against buttoned-up type as a hard-drinking, straight-talking, tanned-to-a-crisp matriarch), blurts out her secret to Woody. Can he bear to wound his bestie with the knowledge that Matthew might not share DNA with the late father he idolized? But there are a lot of other things going on. Too many, really. When Woody, his wife Joy (Brittany Ishibashi), and their two daughters roll up to the McConaugheys’ Austin estate in a psychedelic bus, it’s because Joy refuses to let her husband abandon their eldest child, Olive (Highdee Kuan), as she heals from a broken engagement. Matthew has his own family and challenges, writing a speech for a public event honoring his dad while trying to nurture a bond with his kids, especially timid teenage son Wyatt (Nolan Almeida). Brothers is most generic in these double-family-sitcom storylines, with the two wild, larger-than-life leads constantly testing the patience of their more grounded, long-suffering, emotionally intelligent wives. A subplot for, perhaps, True Detective fans, in which two sickly old-timer crooks (Jeff Kober and Barry Corbin) try to extract from Woody a debt his dad owed them, feels like excerpts from a different show. What actually works is, not surprisingly, the buddy-comedy stuff—the sillier, the better. Matthew and Woody squished into a rideshare with too many rowdy, young fans? A treat. Woody and Matthew donning ridiculous Sunset Strip rocker disguises, complete with facial prosthetics, to play an acoustic set at a coffee shop? Classic. Showrunner Lee Eisenberg (Stick, The Office), who created Brothers alongside McConaughey, Harrelson, and Schitt’s Creek alum David West Read, understands his leads’ personae, and they’re more than game to laugh at themselves. The series opens by showing us the versions that live in the public imagination; Woody enters smoking a joint and holding a surfboard, while Matthew roams the sun-dappled fields of Texas, sipping whisky in a cowboy hat as he recites an excerpt from his megahit memoir Greenlights in voiceover. He plays the charismatic, image-conscious, golden-boy straight man to Woody’s free-thinking hippie, a guy who doesn’t let fame stop him being as weird as he wants to be. Matthew McConaughey, left, and Woody Harrelson in Brothers Apple TV Then it pushes those archetypes without fully puncturing them. Woody’s discovery that they might literally be brothers from different mothers adds some useful pressure—though, truth be told, the storyline is less effective as a driver of plot than it is as a showcase for Taylor, who disappears into her role, making Ma Mac a spitfire but never a cartoon. Brothers gets more juice out of another ripped-from-the-headlines narrative: McConaughey’s political ambitions. While in real life he decided, after months of speculation, not to enter the 2022 Texas gubernatorial race, the show puts Matthew on the campaign trail. (What it carefully doesn’t do is align him with any party or cause. But boy does he love Texas!) It’s a fun reality check for a guy who’s great at kissing babies but can’t answer a simple question about housing policy. His jaded campaign manager, played to the hilt by Lisa Gilroy, gets the series’ most self-aware jokes (“Your Achilles’ heel is that you have been a celebrity for so, so long that your brain has turned into a big old sloppy pile of goo”). A cynical, Type A Armando Iannucci character in the land of alright, alright, alright, she’s deployed sparingly enough to add some edge without killing the laid-back vibe. Really, though, the campaign could have been the whole show. It’s the perfect context for contrasting the stars’ personalities, pitting Matthew’s traditionalism and discretion against Woody’s unapologetic outlandishness. When the former announces he’s running, his old friend doesn’t mince words. “I’m a f-cking anarchist, and I think this is the stupidest, craziest f-cking idea you ever had,” Woody exclaims. But will he support Matthew on the trail anyway? Of course he will. Because, regardless of what any DNA test says, that is what makes a brother.

Hence while things are unpleasant at the moment, I think that longer-term this is a Early computers were purpose built and sometimes hard wired to do the job they needed to do. They were replaced with machines that could easily be reprogrammed with punch cards and writing code in machine language, rendering the skills of a whole generation of computer technicians that did that hand wiring largely obsolete. Once human friendly programming languages made their debut I am sure there was much and wringing about the decline of machine language skills and dumbing down of coding, not to mention bl It's been a long slide downward, but AI will accelerate the need for exohertz computers to render a Mandelbrot set in less than a week. I do not think so, at least not with LLMs. The problem is that an LLM does not even begin to reach the level of reliability of a typical compiler. LLMs hallucinate, create bloated code, leave out functionality that was requested, have no clearly specified functionality, cannot deal with subtleties, etc. Hence LLM-generated code needs to be reviewed carefully and that is probably in the end more expensive than having somebody competent write the code from scratch. There are also indications that reviewing LLM code is mind-numbingly boring and people will burn out doing it, while even now there are not enough people that can do it in the first place. Also note that the attempts of replacing programmers are _very_ old. When I studied CS almost 40 years ago, the 5GL project had just resoundingly failed. Its idea was to have program specifications in constraint logic and then have the machine create code for that. The problem was not that this did not work. The problem was that creating a specification on the level required was much harder and more time-consuming that writing the code directly with a typical informal or semi-formal specification. And, quite frankly, I see the same problem with LLMs as translation engine, as soon as you need actual production quality code. For producing low-quality slop, LLMs are fine. But that slop will not survive in the real world, especially as the one form of code LLMs are really good at creating is attack code, which does not need to be reliable, secure or maintainable. Hence low-skill hackers got a major upgrade here, while LLM use makes the defenders even weaker. Onec again, the deeper issue is "the irony of technologies of abundance in the hands of those still thinking in terms of scarcity."https://pdfernhout.net/recogni... [pdfernhout.net]====Military robots like drones are ironic because they are created essentially to force humans to work like robots in an industrialized social order. Why not just create industrial robots to do the work instead? Nuclear weapons are ironic because they are about using space age systems to fight over oil and land. Why not just use advanced materials as found in nuclear missiles to make renewable energy sources (like windmills or solar panels) to replace oil, or why not use rocketry to move into space by building space habitats for more land? Biological weapons like genetically-engineered plagues are ironic because they are about using advanced life-altering biotechnology to fight over which old-fashioned humans get to occupy the planet. Why not just use advanced biotech to let people pick their skin color, or to create living arkologies and agricultural abundance for everyone everywhere? These militaristic socio-economic ironies would be hilarious if they were not so deadly serious. .... Likewise, even United States three-letter agencies like the NSA and the CIA, as well as their foreign counterparts, are becoming ironic institutions in many ways. Despite probably having more computing power per square foot than any other place in the world, they seem not to have thought much about the implications of all that computer power and organized information to transform the world into a place of abundance for all. Cheap computing makes possible just about cheap everything else, as does the ability to make better designs through shared computing. .... There is a fundamental mismatch between 21st century reality and 20th century security thinking. Those "security" agencies are using those tools of abundance, cooperation, and sharing mainly from a mindset of scarcity, competition, and secrecy. Given the power of 21st century technology as an amplifier (including as weapons of mass destruction), a scarcity-based approach to using such technology ultimately is just making us all insecure. Such powerful technologies of abundance, designed, organized, and used from a mindset of scarcity could well ironically doom us all whether through military robots, nukes, plagues, propaganda, or whatever else... Or alternatively, as Bucky Fuller and others have suggested, we could use such technologies to build a world that is abundant and secure for all. ... The big problem is that all these new war machines and the surrounding infrastructure are created with the tools of abundance [otherwise they would not be so powerful or capable of so much rapid destruction as well as rapid creation]. The irony is that these tools of abundance are being wielded by people still obsessed with fighting over scarcity. So, the scarcity-based political mindset driving the military uses the technologies of abundance to create artificial scarcity. That is a tremendously deep irony that remains so far unappreciated by the mainstream. We the people need to redefine security in a sustainable and resilient way. Much current US military doctrine is based around unilateral security ("I'm safe because you are nervous") and extrinsic security ("I'm safe despite long supply lines because I have a bunch of soldiers to defend them"), which both lead to expensive arms races. We need as a society to move to other paradigms like Morton Deutsch's mutual security ("We're all looking out for each other's safety") and Amory Lovin's intrinsic security ("Our redundant decentralized local systems can take a lot of pounding whether from storm, earthquake, or bombs and would still would keep working"). ...==== In 2001 supposedly it really was "human error". This is an example of one of the legitimate challenges facing the increased integration of AI into warfare. The more fundamental problem is distributed responsibility and lack of transparency.Until recently, drone strikes required a long sequence of command decisions, starting with intel gathering, analysis, decision, orders, and remote command of the device. No one is directly responsible when something goes wrong, the failure is distributed among many players with the result that no one has to bear the consequences.For example, the "collateral murder" [wikipedia.org] incident, where apache helicopters gunned down civilians and journalists.In normal situations, we have Derek Chauvin [wikipedia.org], who was an individual who caused the death of one man. He went to trial, the evidence was examined, and guilt was assigned. Trials are more-or-less held in the public light, and journalists and interested parties can examine court records and in some cases reanalyze evidence (not all physical evidence is kept or available, but for example, DNA evidence can sometimes be reexamined).That's how it's supposed to happen.Contrast that with erroneously ordered drone strikes that hit civilians instead of legitimate targets.I suppose the biggest, most egregious example would be George Bush taking the country to war because Saddam Hussein had "weapons of mass destruction", and of course one were found, and the security services swore up and down that they told him there were none, but he stated "well, the message didn't get through".And skated responsibility. This is an example of one of the legitimate challenges facing the increased integration of AI into warfare. The more fundamental problem is distributed responsibility and lack of transparency. Until recently, drone strikes required a long sequence of command decisions, starting with intel gathering, analysis, decision, orders, and remote command of the device. No one is directly responsible when something goes wrong, the failure is distributed among many players with the result that no one has to bear the consequences. For example, the "collateral murder" [wikipedia.org] incident, where apache helicopters gunned down civilians and journalists. In normal situations, we have Derek Chauvin [wikipedia.org], who was an individual who caused the death of one man. He went to trial, the evidence was examined, and guilt was assigned. Trials are more-or-less held in the public light, and journalists and interested parties can examine court records and in some cases reanalyze evidence (not all physical evidence is kept or available, but for example, DNA evidence can sometimes be reexamined). Contrast that with erroneously ordered drone strikes that hit civilians instead of legitimate targets. I suppose the biggest, most egregious example would be George Bush taking the country to war because Saddam Hussein had "weapons of mass destruction", and of course one were found, and the security services swore up and down that they told him there were none, but he stated "well, the message didn't get through". To bring this closer to home, software engineers implement all sorts of insecure, inconvenient, and privacy violating features with no moral pushback. Even if it's completely obvious that something is an anti-feature, people do it because a) their job depends on it, and b) it's someone else's decision. And if there's a breech and ten million social security numbers get made public, the company simply fixes the problem and continues. No responsibility is assigned. We Finally Found Out What It Would Take for Republicans to Abandon Donald Trump
2026-09-23

We Finally Found Out What It Would Take for Republicans to Abandon Donald Trump

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. For more than a decade, Democrats in Congress have wondered what, if anything, would convince their Republican colleagues to drop Donald Trump. Despite private misgivings, GOP elected officials held their noses even as the president insulted veterans, bragged about assaulting women, trafficked in racism, abused his office, and tried to overturn a free and fair election. So far, their loyalty—measured purely by expediency—has been worth it. Despite his many flaws, Trump twice won his party unified control of Washington. But if winning elections is Republicans’ excuse for forgiving Trump, losing them appears to be the line that cannot be crossed. Case in point: the 2026 midterms. As the GOP limps toward November with an unpopular, term-limited president at the helm, candidates in tough races—including several who previously supported Trump without reservation—are starting to highlight their differences with him. It’s both a sign of desperation and a strategic move that could break a Democratic wave. Which Republicans are shying away from Trump? As you might expect, several are in closely divided states where moderation and independence can pay dividends. One is former Rep. Mike Rogers, who’s running for Senate in Michigan and blames Trump’s Iran war for squeezing voters’ pocketbooks. “The war with Iran needs to end, and end quickly,” he said yesterday. In Iowa, Rep. Ashley Hinson has called for an immediate end to the war and avoided campaigning with Vice President J.D. Vance. And last week, two other vulnerable Iowans, Reps. Zach Nunn and Mariannette Miller-Meeks, joined the handful of House Republicans who have voted to halt the fighting. So are Republicans mostly jumping ship over Iran? Not exclusively. Rep. Tom Tiffany, who’s running for Wisconsin governor, recently distanced himself from Trump’s effort to rename Lake Ontario, criticized him for lowering tariffs on beef imports, and flatly affirmed that “Joe Biden won” the 2020 election. Ohio Sen. Jon Husted, who faces a stiff challenge from Democratic former Sen. Sherrod Brown, told the Wall Street Journal that he doesn’t share Trump’s view that Americans who oppose A.I. data centers “want to end up being backwards and poor.” Overall, Republican candidates now talk about the president and his MAGA movement less often than they did earlier this year, a Washington Post survey found. OK, but Republicans have groused about Trump before without doing much else. Why should I take these breaks more seriously? In some cases, the candidates are literally broadcasting them. Rogers made his comments about Iran in a campaign video. Others are distancing themselves on issues that used to be solidly in the president’s wheelhouse. Last week, Florida Rep. María Elvira Salazar said in an ad that Trump’s approach to immigration enforcement has “gone too far.” Even Republicans who should be campaigning on safe ground are looking nervous. On Tuesday, the Miami Herald reported that Rep. Byron Donalds, who’s running for governor in solidly red Florida, had scoured prominent Trump references from his campaign website. Other defectors can’t even point to political expedience. Sen. John Curtis of Utah isn’t up for reelection until 2030. But on Tuesday he said the Senate should investigate whether Donald Trump Jr. is trading on his father’s name following revelations that a Russian oligarch helped pay for his wedding. But isn’t distancing from an unpopular president pretty normal? Similar things have happened to Democratic presidents who found themselves underwater come midterm season. In 2010, which famously turned into a shellacking for Barack Obama, many Democratic candidates shied away from his agenda. Biden got a similar treatment in 2022. But what makes these defections notable is that Republicans are doing it to Trump, who is infamously thin-skinned and treats political tactics like personal slights. Mostly with denial. “President Trump is the unequivocal leader, best messenger, and unmatched motivator for the Republican Party,” a White House spokesperson told Politico this week, despite polls showing even GOP voters souring on his handling of Iran, inflation, and other issues. The president, whose approval ratings have hit all-time lows in some surveys, has also tried deflection. “They’re not angry at me, but they are angry at Republicans,” he said of voters last month. Some Republicans who have offered Trump constructive criticism claim he’s listening. On Sunday, Salazar recalled a pleasant conversation she had with the president after warning that Hispanic voters “feel betrayed” by his immigration agenda. “He was a gentleman, very courteous, very receptive,” Salazar said. We’ll see if that lasts. Obvious question: Will this actually work? Distancing from an unpopular president helped some Democratic midterm candidates win in red and purple states during the Obama and Biden years. Republicans, meanwhile, whiffed several winnable races in 2018 and 2022 because their candidates hewed too closely to Trump, proof that not breaking from a floundering commander in chief can cost seats (even if Trump later helped Republicans retake power). On the other hand, the fervor of Trump’s base makes crossing him risky, and voters rarely reward Johnny-come-latelys. Earlier this year, Rogers was extolling the president’s Iran war as “a mission for safety and justice” and downplaying Michiganders’ anger over gas prices. Hinson has repeatedly voted against House resolutions that would’ve ended the war, including last week. Tiffany tried to help Trump reverse Biden’s 2020 victory. Throwing the president under the bus is one thing. But for Republicans belatedly trying to backpedal, the bigger problem may be that they were for Trump before they were against him. In honor of Slate’s 30th anniversary, my colleagues have been revisiting some of their favorite stories from over the years. For your evening entertainment, here are a few of them. For the perfectionists: Back in 2011, Farhad Manjoo argued that “you should never, ever use two spaces after a period.” His typographical cri de coeur has entered the annals of classic Slate pitches. For the sci-fi fans: Did you know that Slate used to publish speculative fiction? Dive into this short story about humans’ first contact with an alien who’s been corrupted by some very modern vices. For the romantics: Emily Yoffe’s essay “My Husband’s Other Wife” is a poignant ode to her husband’s first wife, who died of breast cancer tragically young. Political Gabfest host Emily Bazelon praised the essay’s unflinching candor in exploring her relationship, noting that “it’s the honesty that makes me cry.” For the detectives: There was a period of time when you could not escape “Baby Shark.” The kids’ song became a worldwide hit in 2019—but that wasn’t the first time this earworm went viral. Willa Paskin investigated the multidecade rise of “Baby Shark” in a great episode of Decoder Ring. If you’re a true Slate fanatic, head over to our special archival homepage for even more blasts from the past. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The Supreme Court Is Poised to Strangle Climate Lawsuits in the Cradle
2026-09-22

The Supreme Court Is Poised to Strangle Climate Lawsuits in the Cradle

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Who bears the costs of climate change? The answer is very simple: you, me, and most acutely of all, low-income households and marginalized people. This Supreme Court term will open in just over a week with a case that will help decide whether that is to remain the sole case, or if carbon polluters might actually bear some burden for the effects of human-caused climate change, for which they are largely responsible. The case, Suncor Energy v. County Commissioners of Boulder County, questions whether local and state governments can sue oil companies in state court and force them to pay some of the localized costs of climate change. On this week’s Amicus podcast, Dahlia Lithwick asked Sam Sankar, vice president of programs at Earthjustice, to lay out the stakes. Their conversation has been edited and condensed for clarity. Dahlia Lithwick: Before we turn to the precise contours of Suncor, I think it’s worth laying out how we can follow the trail of climate denialism and suspicion of regulatory agencies right to the doors of SCOTUS and a legal apparatus that gears up in order to serve a deregulatory agenda targeting environmental protections. Sam Sankar: Broadly speaking, the Supreme Court left alone environmental law in the early days, back in the 1970s. The D.C. Circuit was the one that was really making tons of law around these areas, tons of doctrines and administrative law to guide the agencies without blocking them. The first shot in that denialism and suspicion of regulatory agencies comes in early cases about standing, where Justice Antonin Scalia starts drawing lines and saying, You know, you can’t just sue about anything. You’ve really got to be personally injured in a very clear and concrete way. That kind of requirement itself makes it tricky to sue about everything from taxpayer issues to, as you know, Donald Trump blowing up the White House and rebuilding it in his image, or the environment. The next thing I think you start seeing is the court becoming interested in, despite its professed adherence to textualism, reading these statutes with an eyebrow raised. Could they really mean that? It seems like there’s a lot going on here. There might even be major questions about these regulations that are coming out. For those who aren’t aware, “major questions” is a doctrine the court announced a couple of years ago that seems tailor-made to restrict the ability of agencies to regulate. What we’re now seeing is a lot of these decisions around preemption. Because we know the federal government isn’t doing what needs to happen, many progressive states are trying to do things on their own. And the court is being asked to block state action, it’s being asked to say: Federal law, even if it’s not really being implemented, blocks all of that stuff. And that has happened in tandem with an extraordinarily conservative Supreme Court coming into being under the banner of ideas like textualism and originalism and supposedly neutral approaches to interpreting the law. And so this court is now really openly hostile to the idea of regulation and is reading statutes to limit regulatory power. If you look at the terms of these old statutes, for years the court was reading them literally and saying: You’ve got a lot of space here. Now, in cases like Sackett, about the Clean Water Act, they’re saying: We’re going to read it differently. I want to follow up on what you said about standing doctrine and the environmental cases. A couple of weeks ago, we had Erwin Chemerinsky, dean of Berkeley Law, on the show, and he was raising the alarm about the potential catastrophic effects of the White House ballroom case on environmental law. Dean Chemerinsky said that when the justices pooh-poohed and batted aside aesthetic injury claims, that really raised questions for standing in a massive amount of environmental lawsuits. Did it raise the same alarms for you? If you read the legal logic of that opinion, it’s absolutely—I won’t say terrifying—it’s of deep concern, really problematic, because a lot of environmental law is about protecting not just public health, but the environment for people—beautiful wild spaces, animals, rivers that are pleasant to recreate on, and even just the smog and haze that blanket a lot of cities. So even Justice Scalia, while he narrowed standing doctrine, left open very clearly the idea that people who had aesthetic injuries because they went to places regularly and because they were seeing them degraded, that was absolutely a reason why you could have standing. Now he didn’t make it easy to prove it, but absolutely, aesthetic injury was real. The ballroom case seems inconsistent with that. What I am hoping is that this was another one of those “Donald Trump is different” cases. I think that all of us are hopeful that that really is just good for the one case. Having said that, that’s not stopping anybody from citing that case in environmental law cases. You saw a flurry of Rule 28(j) letters—that’s the letter that you send out to alert a court you’re in about a decision from another court after briefs have been filed or before oral argument. Those letters went out like mad to all kinds of courts that were dealing with standing in environmental cases. OK, let’s talk about Suncor—which is not your case—but it is an incredibly significant case about an actual thing that we call climate change. It’s really important because it’s part of an array of dozens of cases filed over many years by state and local governments who are trying to rush into the void and hold the fossil fuel industry accountable for climate change. Before we talk about the complexity of it, I would love for you to just tell us the history of the case and what the issue is. This is one of a couple of dozen cases like this around the country. Boulder County’s claim follows a path similar to many of the other ones, although not identical. What Boulder County said was: No. 1, climate change is real and it has been real for decades, but through those decades the oil industry has been denying the risks of its products, denying climate change, denying a link between its products and climate change, and basically suppressing the public’s understanding of the risks of the product and suppressing any kind of action meant to regulate it or control it. That deception injured consumers. No. 2, the products themselves were injurious to consumers as well. This claim was brought by a municipality. The municipality wasn’t saying, “We want you to stop drilling for oil, or get rid of gas powered vehicles.” What it was saying was: We want damages for this because we’re spending a ton of money dealing with climate change. We’re having to reinforce roads to deal with extreme heat or extreme precipitation. We’ve got to rebuild infrastructure to deal with hundred-year floods that are happening every other year now. We’re dealing with heat stress and heat stroke and all the things that the people in our town are being affected by. We’ve never had wildfire seasons like this in the past. It’s very hard for us to deal with, and we’re spending a ton of money to upgrade systems, to fight the fires, or even to deal with the aftermath of these things. Boulder County estimated the money that it had shelled out for this to be around $100 million, but they haven’t been specific about the dollar amount they want, it’s just they’re saying they want to recover that from some of these folks. Now, this case isn’t over yet. Nowhere near over. What’s happened is Boulder has made allegations about the deception, it’s made allegations about the link between the products and climate change, and it’s made allegations about the responsibility the oil companies should bear as a result of that. These are questions that get resolved by juries every day. All Boulder County is looking for right now is the chance to prove these claims to a jury. What Suncor and Exxon are trying to do right now is stop the case from going forward. What they’re saying is that this kind of case shouldn’t even get off the ground. There shouldn’t be a trial, there shouldn’t be any kind of judgment. They should be let off the hook before the trial begins. The Supreme Court has stepped in at an incredibly early stage in this litigation. Nothing’s been decided yet. And this then suggests that this will become another hypertechnical doctrinal vehicle that’s going to be used not just to short-circuit this litigation, which, as we’ve both stipulated, has barely come off the blocks, but it could ensure that no one gets to the merits in any of the similar cases around the country. That has been the trick that the court has been using, right? Using in this case preemption, in others standing, using whatever it is that they can to make sure that the case never gets through the courthouse doors and never gets litigated on the merits. I just want you to amplify how important it is when you slam the courthouse doors, whether it’s preemption or standing, in environment-land, that’s it. There’s not another plaintiff that’s going to come around with a better injury, if the federal regulation in this case bigfoots all over state law such that nobody can ever bring a lawsuit. That’s the end. Well it’s worse even than you’re describing, for two reasons. First, yes, if the Supreme Court ruled that this case can’t go forward, there would be many cases that would be probably fatally injured. Now, Boulder County’s claims, which are primarily about risky products and deception, aren’t identical to all the cases. Michigan has a case, I believe, that has more to do with antitrust law. But it’s going to be awfully hard in those cases to dodge a broadly worded Supreme Court opinion that basically says climate change is not a local thing, only the federal government can do it, even if the federal government isn’t doing anything. The other problem is the Supreme Court thinks it knows the story right now. And that story goes something like: It wasn’t really a big-deal deception. Come on, the oil companies didn’t know everything, really, and who can blame them. During the early days of the tobacco lawsuits, the tobacco companies were declaring up, down, and sideways that they didn’t know any of this, that they were just doing the best they could. It took investigations, trials, discovery, all of the tools of modern litigation, to get documents, interview witnesses, to do the fact-building that exposed just how bad the industry had been behaving. There’s still a lot for us to learn about what the oil industry did and didn’t know, even though we have some pretty damning stuff already, and about what it did and didn’t do. It’s only fair to the plaintiffs to give them the chance to show the truth and then let the jury decide. And then whatever the jury decides, the Supreme Court can then review it on a full record—a full record being the transcript of the trial, all the evidence that comes in, all of that stuff, the Supreme Court then can review a package, a full picture. Rather than saying, as I suspect they are poised to do now, “We know the answer,” before you even get started. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. If you want to block ads but still support Slate, consider subscribing. Already a member? Sign in here
News and Politics - Slate Magazine
The Reaction to Trump’s Speech at the U.N. Was Revealing
2026-09-22

The Reaction to Trump’s Speech at the U.N. Was Revealing

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Over the past year, Donald Trump’s actions on the world stage have ushered in a new era, in which most of the world’s leaders realize they no longer need to pretend to respect the president of the United States. Their reception of his address to the U.N. General Assembly on Tuesday made clear that this era has firmly taken hold. His 38-minute address was interrupted just four times for mild applause—when he said that Iran will never have a nuclear weapon, that all of Israel’s hostages had been freed from Gaza, and that no international bureaucracy will pass a tax (as if this would have a chance of happening), and to thank his wife, Melania, for heading a group that recovered Ukrainian children who had been kidnapped by Russia. His previous U.N. speeches have been jammed with bluster and bunkum, but none more so than this one. He began with boasts that seemed meant for a domestic audience—and that were clearly false to everyone in the room. “Our country is stronger today than ever before”—this, as the U.S. military is failing to subdue Iran, a third-rate power, despite firing bombs and missiles at more than 15,000 targets. “Our economy is the envy of the world”—this, as the rest of the world maneuvers to withdraw assets from U.S. markets and seek alternative supply chains. “The entire world is being lifted by the wealth, strength, spirit, and momentum of a newly energized and very powerful United States of America”—when, in fact, the world is suffering from an energy crisis created by Trump’s unnecessary war, which has moreover revealed weaknesses in our military arsenal. Trump also said, “We inherited a dark age of America from the previous administration, and we turned it into the golden age of America”—a claim that no president should have to make if it were true, and that anyone looking at any data would know is not true. The speech was strewn with falsehoods that must have appalled even sympathetic listeners in the chamber. Of Iran: “They were the bully of the Middle East, but they are the bully no more.” Tell that to shippers who still refuse to send their cargo into the Strait of Hormuz. Or to the Saudis, whose gateway for oil exports—and even their capital city—was just attacked by Iranian-backed Houthis. He claimed that Iran ignored his offers to negotiate a deal before he launched his air assaults seven months ago. But in fact, just days before the raids began, Iran offered to suspend enriching its uranium for three years, to limit enrichment afterward to very low levels, and to allow international inspectors to verify compliance. When it came to the problem of Iran, he claimed, “many presidents and many others just wanted to ignore it.” Never mind that President Barack Obama, along with the leaders of five other countries, secured a 159-page accord that was well on its way to dismantling Iran’s nuclear program, until Trump scuttled it and reimposed sanctions in 2018. That move, which had been encouraged by Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu (who didn’t want Iran reentering the global economy), spurred Iran to restart its program. Even so, Trump once again muddied his own waters by claiming that Operation Midnight Hammer—his bombing raid on three enriched-uranium sites in June 2025—“obliterated” Iran’s nuclear program. In that case, why did he need to start a full-scale war in February 2026—a war, by the way, that has attacked no additional sites related to nuclear weapons? And yet, he told his audience of world leaders, he now has “a big decision to make.” Should he make a deal with Iran—something that he believes will happen after the midterm elections—“or do I annihilate the Islamic republic, and do it quickly? … Do I drive them into hell with no chance of survival?” What sort of world leader, besides a madman, talks like this? And to what end? Annihilation implies killing much of the Iranian population (it’s hard to separate the regime from its people), which Trump said at the start of the war he wanted to protect. He then turned from bloodthirsty threats to a reprise of his self-congratulatory bit about ending eight wars, taking it several steps further by reading aloud the letters of thanks that leaders of Pakistan, Armenia, Cambodia, and other countries sent him praising his “extraordinary statesmanship” and “innovative diplomacy.” First, only a very insecure man feels the need to recite rave reviews of his performance on the world (or any other) stage. Second, as others have noted, the claim is exaggerated at best. Some of those wars are still going on; others Trump played little or no role in settling. He said an end to the Russia–Ukraine war—which he thought at the start of the term would be easy to solve, thanks to his belief that he’s great friends with Vladimir Putin—“is going to happen quicker than people understand.” He made the same casually confident claim about ending the war with Iran, saying he “will definitely get it done, one way or the other.” He also boasted of the deal—which he would be signing later that day—securing U.S. security in Greenland, ignoring (perhaps not knowing) that the deal is pretty much a reaffirmation of a treaty signed in 1951 (and still in effect). If his boasts mean he will no longer threaten to annex Greenland, that’s good. But the deal is nothing new. Meanwhile, he blamed “cowards and traitors” for perpetrating the hoax that the war has left the U.S. military low on vital munitions—when, in fact, this was the conclusion of a recent report by his own Defense Department’s inspector general. He claimed that his attacks on alleged drug boats coming out of Venezuela have destroyed 97 percent of the narcotics coming into the region by sea. In fact, the Drug Enforcement Administration has concluded that the strikes—which killed more than 200 people (some possibly real drug traffickers, some fishermen, some both)—have not reduced the flow of drugs into the U.S. He lauded Secretary of State Marco Rubio for organizing a deal that would “fundamentally change” (read: overthrow) the “failing state” of Cuba, claiming that the regime is supported by “left-wing radicals” here at home, such as the Communist Party of the United States of America, antifa, and the Democratic Socialists of America. This is nuts. To paraphrase what the great radical protester Saul Alinsky once said of the New Left, the CPUSA couldn’t organize a luncheon, much less a revolution. Antifa is largely mythical. And the DSA? The day before, Trump chummily met, for the third time, with New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani, America’s most visible and popular democratic socialist, wishing him well and congratulating him on his accomplishments. Winding into the stretch, Trump pissed on his hosts’ doorstep, condemning the U.N. for “imposing the far-left agenda of global bureaucrats.” He also called for the dismantlement of the International Criminal Court, which he characterized as an “anti-America tribunal,” an “evil group of people,” and a “rogue institution.” He then mocked one of the main topics of this year’s General Assembly—an effort to put restraints on artificial intelligence, which Trump said should be called superintelligence, because the word artificial makes it sound “fake.” He claimed that A.I.’s fearmongers are “the same people” who said global warming would kill everybody in 12 years and who ranted about “the Russia, Russia, Russia hoax.” But no experts have made such drastic claims about global warming, and some of the biggest worriers about A.I. include some of the Silicon Valley scientists who helped create it. Trump sees A.I. as a purely benevolent economic engine, saying, “We’re leading now over China by a lot … and we’re going to keep it that way”—no doubt disappointing those who hoped he and Xi Jinping might work out some rules of the road at their summit in Washington later this week. He added, “I’m not going to stifle the growth of something that will be bigger than the Industrial Revolution.” If dangers arise, he said, they can be handled by the Department of Justice—which might be the funniest thing Trump has ever said. He concluded on a note of sheer mendacity, given what he had said before, calling on his fellow leaders to “build a future for our people that is brighter, more hopeful, more magnificent than ever before. God bless the nations of the world”—except, presumably, the ones he’s thinking about annihilating—and “God bless America.” The good news about Trump’s speech is that no one had any reason to pay any attention to it. It came off as the empty bombast of a declining leader who thinks—or perhaps just pretends to think—that he’s still in control. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Southern Water taps fiber network to sniff out leaky pipes
2026-09-23

Southern Water taps fiber network to sniff out leaky pipes

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Another utility sees the light in putting existing telecoms infrastructure to work UK utility provider Southern Water is turning fiber-optic telecoms cables into underground sensors in an effort to find leaks in its water network. The privatized biz is trialing tech from Lightsonic that uses Openreach's fiber network to detect leaking pipes. Openreach - BT's infrastructure arm - and Affinity Water, which supplies parts of southern England, tested the system in March. As The Register reported at the time, the system analyzes tiny changes in light traveling through fiber-optic cables to detect vibrations caused by nearby underground leaks. Machine learning helps distinguish those signals from traffic, roadworks and other background noise, and estimates where the water is escaping. Lightsonic says the resulting location data allows engineers to investigate and repair leaks more quickly. It claims the tech is already preventing the loss of more than four million liters (about a million gallons) of water per day across 650 km (400 miles) of the UK's water network. Southern Water is adopting the technology after one of Britain's hottest and driest summers on record, with almost three-quarters of England officially under drought conditions. Southern England is among the country's most water-stressed regions.The utility said Lightsonic's work with Affinity Water and Openreach influenced its decision to run the trial. Southern Water hopes the trial will help it meet Ofwat's leakage-reduction targets for 2030 and strengthen its drought resilience by easing pressure on reservoirs and water abstraction. The company provides water and wastewater services across parts of Hampshire, the Isle of Wight, West Sussex, East Sussex, and Kent. "We've reduced leakage across our network for three consecutive years and continue to invest in innovative techniques and technologies to find and fix leaks even more effectively," said Wayne Novelli, Southern Water's leakage and network strategy manager. "We've been impressed by the leakage reduction metrics and proven success of Lightsonic's technology and are looking forward to working with the team to trial a new way of detecting and tackling them." Southern Water's efforts to conserve clean water come against a less flattering record on wastewater. A court this week fined it £2.4 million ($3.1 million) over several pollution incidents in Kent. That followed a £7.1 million ($9.4 million) fine in July for illegal sewage discharges that forced beaches in Thanet to close. Swedish celebs campaign for public rudeness ... to prevent cyber scams
2026-09-23

Swedish celebs campaign for public rudeness ... to prevent cyber scams

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
The campaign follows a highly profitable year for crooks targeting the over-60s Swedish celebrities will front a national campaign encouraging the public to exercise their rudeness in an effort to drive down rates of cyber scams. According to Polisen, Sweden’s police authority, the country’s cybercriminals profited 5.7 billion SEK ($579.9 million USD) in 2025 alone, and their scams are especially successful when targeting the elderly. “The message is simple: Dare to be rude – it could save you from being scammed,” said the Polisen this week, announcing a relaunch of the campaign with four new faces. Actors Suzanne Reuter, Lennart Jähkel, and Christina Schollin are joined by journalist Claes Elfsberg in the public awareness campaign, which will run through October 11 across print media, social media, and television. “I am part of the police campaign because it is a terrible crime that affects so many people,” said Reuter. “If I can use my voice to help someone not to be scammed, then I want to do it. “Many of us are raised to always be polite and accommodating, and there are many in my generation. But today you have to dare to be a little more questioning. It's not rude – it's self-protection.” Polisen’s national operations leader, Robert Öhman, said fraudsters are skilled social engineers, adept at building trust simultaneously with a sense of stress or urgency. His aim for the campaign is to encourage the public to feel more comfortable with simply hanging up the phone if they feel something might be awry. Telephone-based scams are by far the most common. Typically targeting the over-60s, the Polisen said scammers will often send targets SMS messages under various guises, but always claiming to represent a trusted authority, such as the government or a bank. Unwitting victims then call the telephone number included in the message and are connected to the scammers who read from a prepared script. Like the Netherlands, Sweden is also affected by fake police officer scams. These typically see crooks dress as police officers, offering to collect and safely store valuables, but steal them after the victim transfers them. In these cases, police and campaigners are urging the public to fight their learned social norms and shut the door “at the slightest hesitation.” “The police and other reputable organizations or companies never go to someone's home to collect gold, credit cards or other valuables,” the Polisen said. Officials are not only concerned about the personal losses incurred by victims, but also about the ways in which the stolen funds are used. “The money that the fraudsters get can also help finance other serious crimes,” said Öhman. “In the ongoing operation, we can confirm close cooperation between the organized fraudsters and the pure violent networks. “Therefore, we must both reduce the risk of people being deceived and strike at the criminal actors behind the crimes.” Swedish police and the country’s Post and Telecom Agency (PTS) started working more closely to combat telephone fraud in June. That same month, the Swedish Parliament approved new rules under the Electronic Communications Act compelling providers of number-based services to block suspected fraud communications before they reach the recipient. The amendments came into force as of August 1, and followed previous legislative tweaks that tasked the PTS with blocking spam calls, especially those originating from overseas and spoofing real Swedish numbers. ® Regulator wants to know whether parent Aylo did its homework before reopening the door to UK iPhone users PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Another utility sees the light in putting existing telecoms infrastructure to work Kids can learn why BGP matters in a semester, but that won’t leave them ready to implement it The campaign follows a highly profitable year for crooks targeting the over-60s Developers test what they can build with TypeSafe's fast, typed decision model Anthropic and OpenAI debut Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Sol and Luna 'first' publicly documented Windows implant to use LLMs for C2 China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws Did Copilot write the synchronization code? Whisky merchant Master of Malt confirms customer data spilt
2026-09-23

Whisky merchant Master of Malt confirms customer data spilt

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Attackers had four days to drink in names, addresses, emails and phone numbers Hackers have raided Master of Malt's customer database after a compromised ecommerce app gave them four days to help themselves to names, addresses, phone numbers, and email addresses. The online booze retailer began notifying customers this week after learning that Ribon, an app connected to its BigCommerce store, had been compromised. According to an email sent to customers, seen by The Register, attackers got hold of a BigCommerce application key held by Ribon and used it to access customer data between September 13 and 17. "I'm sorry to say that the attackers had access to your name, email address, phone number, and address," Master of Malt founder Justin Petszaft told customers. Passwords, credit card details, and other payment information escaped the raid, with Master of Malt saying they are stored in a separate system that was not compromised. Ribon is owned, managed, and operated by Be A Part Of, which Master of Malt says describes itself as a Fastr brand, a corporate family tree best tackled before the whisky. Master of Malt said BigCommerce alerted it to the incident, telling the retailer that Ribon had been hacked. According to the notification, the attackers compromised an application key held by Ribon and were then able to use it to gain access to customer data. Master of Malt said BigCommerce's security team uninstalled the affected app the same day and "assured us there is no ongoing compromise and no further customer data can be accessed." The Register has asked BigCommerce how many merchants and customers were affected, what access the compromised Ribon key provided, how it was stolen, and whether any other third-party applications were affected. We have not yet received a response. Master of Malt is now warning customers that the stolen information could be put to use in phishing emails, spam, and scam phone calls. "Please be extra vigilant against potential phone calls, spam and phishing attacks targeting you using the stolen data, and question anyone asking you to click a link or share data," Petszaft said. Master of Malt says it won't ask for passwords or payment details over email or phone, so anyone receiving such a request should treat it with suspicion and contact the retailer directly instead. Master of Malt has also set up a page where it says it will publish additional technical details and further updates rather than repeatedly emailing affected customers. For anyone caught up in the breach, the whisky might now be the least dangerous thing arriving from Master of Malt. ® Updated at 11.21 UTC on September 23, 2026, to add: A spokesperson at Big Commerce made contact after this article was published to say: "On September 17, 2026, Commerce confirmed that API credentials belonging to third-party applications Ribon and Ribon 1.5, owned and operated by “Be A Part Of,” a Fastr company, had been compromised due to a Fastr system compromise. "The credentials were used to inject malicious scripts into a small number of merchant storefronts. This was not a breach of Commerce systems or the BigCommerce platform. While the Ribon applications are third-party apps independently installed by the merchant where the relationship occurs between the merchant and the third-party application, Commerce acted in the best interest of our customers and their shoppers by uninstalling the application from affected stores to revoke the attacker’s access and limit harm, notifying affected merchants directly, and providing log data to support the developer’s own investigation." BT Tower's rooftop pool plan brings swimmers back down to earth
2026-09-23

BT Tower's rooftop pool plan brings swimmers back down to earth

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
MCR's hotel proposal puts facility on a lower building, while public access to upper floors set to be restored London's iconic BT Tower is set to get a rooftop swimming pool in plans submitted by the developer, but it may not be quite the view that readers expect. MCR Hotels has submitted its planning application to Camden Council more than two years after BT agreed to sell the Fitzrovia landmark for £275 million ($346 million at the time). The proposal covers the Grade II-listed tower and the lower buildings around its base, with plans for a new public square, shops, restaurants, and a rooftop pool. When The Register reported the pool plans earlier this year, we imagined an infinity edge high up the 177 m tower and a swim with a vertigo-inducing view. The developer's images bring us back down to earth: bathers would look up at the tower from the adjoining rooftop. MCR says the public would be able to use the pool. Those hoping for the view from the top needn't despair. MCR also plans to reopen the tower's upper floors to the public for the first time in decades. The application follows two rounds of public consultation, during which the project team spoke to more than 300 attendees and received feedback that has helped to shape the proposals. "Our vision has always been to honour the BT Tower's remarkable history while reimagining its future. Submitting our planning application is an exciting step towards reopening this landmark after almost 50 years, revitalising the area with new jobs and open space for Camden," said MCR chairman and CEO Tyler Morse. Originally known as the Post Office Tower, it was completed in 1964 to carry microwave antennas that relayed telecom signals between London and the rest of the country. As we reported previously, there was an urban myth that the BT Tower was omitted from maps because it was said to be designated an official secret, owing to its central importance to the UK's national telecoms network. This myth persisted, despite Ordnance Survey maps from the 1970s and 1980s clearly marking the structure. If Camden approves the plans, work is expected to begin in 2029, after BT vacates the site, with a public reopening targeted for 2033. ® Regulator wants to know whether parent Aylo did its homework before reopening the door to UK iPhone users PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Another utility sees the light in putting existing telecoms infrastructure to work Kids can learn why BGP matters in a semester, but that won’t leave them ready to implement it The campaign follows a highly profitable year for crooks targeting the over-60s Developers test what they can build with TypeSafe's fast, typed decision model Anthropic and OpenAI debut Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Sol and Luna 'first' publicly documented Windows implant to use LLMs for C2 China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws Did Copilot write the synchronization code? UK’s uncrewed experimental sub shows it can fire torpedoes
2026-09-23

UK’s uncrewed experimental sub shows it can fire torpedoes

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
XV Excalibur hailed as first US or UK underwater drone to let one off in tests A British uncrewed submarine has test-fired a torpedo for the first time, demonstrating the ability of undersea drones to attack targets as well as perform reconnaissance and surveillance roles. The XV Excalibur, a 12 meter (40 ft) experimental uncrewed underwater vehicle (UUV), was unveiled last year as the Royal Navy’s first large submarine drone as part of Project CETUS. According to the Prime Minister's Office, the test firing was part of the AUKUS program, a trilateral security partnership between Australia, the UK and America. It involved the Excalibur launching a heavyweight torpedo “jointly developed by the US and Australia,” which implies it was the Mk 48 torpedo currently carried by all US Navy submarines. Also according to Downing Street, this was the first time either the US or UK has launched a torpedo from an underwater drone, rather than a crewed submarine. The Register understands that  Excalibur navigated autonomously to its launch position and carried out a pre-programmed launch sequence, but was not responsible for detecting or tracking the target, as target data was provided through external systems. As noted by defense site Navy Lookout, the Excalibur has no torpedo tubes, and its payload bay in the midships section of the hull is too small to accommodate a large torpedo, so it is possible the weapon was carried via some sort of cradle attached to the underside of the sub. We asked the Ministry of Defence for further details, and whether trials were also planned using the British Spearfish torpedo in place of the Mk 48. The builders of the vehicle, Plymouth-based MSubs Ltd, announced in July that it had been awarded a new £6.7 million ($8.9m) contract by the Submarine Delivery Agency to undertake the next phase of trials and evaluations of Excalibur for the Royal Navy. While Excalibur is currently an experimental vehicle, it is expected to inform requirements for future UUVs that the Royal Navy wants to help patrol the North Atlantic and protect against expected Russian sabotage of undersea cables and other infrastructure in the event of hostilities breaking out. The Excalibur tests come as UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham is expected to confirm the UK and US are working together in an AI and Autonomy partnership. This - so we are told - will help Britain protect critical national infrastructure, from sea lanes to air space, providing the means to detect threats and deter adversaries using the latest AI technology developed on both sides of the Atlantic. “We are at our strongest when we work with our partners to tackle the most complex shared challenges we face, driven by our values,” Burnham said. “That ambition is underlined in our new defence partnership with the United States on AI and autonomy, bringing together the brightest minds to strengthen the protection of critical infrastructure, deter those who threaten our security, and use the enormous potential of AI to our advantage.” ® Regulator wants to know whether parent Aylo did its homework before reopening the door to UK iPhone users PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Another utility sees the light in putting existing telecoms infrastructure to work Kids can learn why BGP matters in a semester, but that won’t leave them ready to implement it The campaign follows a highly profitable year for crooks targeting the over-60s Developers test what they can build with TypeSafe's fast, typed decision model Anthropic and OpenAI debut Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Sol and Luna 'first' publicly documented Windows implant to use LLMs for C2 China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws Did Copilot write the synchronization code? www.theregister.com - Articles
Why security belongs in the network
2026-09-23

Why security belongs in the network

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
SPONSORED EXPLAINER: Merging security into the network makes enterprise protection more agile Every attack leaves a trail across the network, from initial reconnaissance to lateral movement and data exfiltration. That makes the network one of an organization’s richest sources of security intelligence. But visibility is only half the story. Because the network connects every user, device, application, and workload, it is also the natural place to verify identity, apply consistent policy, and contain suspicious activity close to its source. The result is faster detection, stronger enforcement, and a smaller blast radius. So, what does it take to make security an integral part of the network, and where should organizations begin? Integrated network security takes security functions that traditionally sat outside the network and embeds them directly into the network fabric to simplify and sharpen protection in the enterprise. Routers, switches, and access points become cybersecurity defenders that identify and prevent threats rather than simply routing and filtering traffic. Enterprises face a gap as the network perimeter dissolves. The assets that used to reside inside the headquarters LAN have scattered everywhere, from the cloud through to edge-based equipment and on-premises servers. Bolting more security tools onto your infrastructure to protect those assets isn't sustainable. License costs increase, tools overlap and sometimes conflict with each other, or they leave non-obvious gaps through which attackers can pass. This fragmented approach to security also involves different teams working at different speeds. Coordination is slow and difficult, hindering the security effort and driving up costs. Unifying security and networking together offers several benefits: Building security directly into the network itself lets you encode a standard approach that works everywhere across the organization and covers everything that connects to the network, (so, in other words, everything you use). This means that you can apply the same policy across the entire network, maintaining them without worrying about fragmentation or policy drift. Agility has become even more important in the AI era. Attackers now use this technology to move more quickly and at scale. Being able to implement and change policies centrally that ripple throughout the organization means that you can adapt to this fast-changing security landscape. Operating from a single source of truth also eliminates the need to stitch together insights from multiple sources. This "implement once run everywhere" capability lets you encode zero-trust principles into the foundation of the system. NIST has a standard for this - SP 800-207 - which promises protection at the asset level rather than the traditional and flawed "one authentication and you're in everywhere" VPN approach. Zero trust isn't so much an individual product as a complete security discipline, and it's hard to implement piecemeal using different solutions. Instead, putting it at the foundation of the network makes it more immediately and ubiquitously workable. Some zero-trust security measures also belong in the network. One example is micro-segmentation, which closes off parts of the network at a granular level to avoid lateral movement and limit the blast radius from any attack. Network-based security also supports robust security for other complex disciplines such as SASE and SD-WAN. Having a common fabric for networking and security enables network and security teams to work closely together. These teams have traditionally operated at a distance, and the gap between them has been valuable for attackers. The less daylight there is between these two functions, the more likely you are to stop intruders. AI is important in two ways when integrating security into the network. First, AI has also become integrated into the network. Machine learning helps to spot patterns in network activity, using them as the feedstock for AIOps systems that predict problems and maintain network reliability and performance. AI has also proven itself to be a valuable security mechanism. That same pattern recognition and predictive capability enables AI to head off attacks before they become a problem. Using AI to support network and security teams enables them to do more with less by delegating routine work so that they can focus on more sophisticated work. Attackers are also using AI to augment their assaults on organizations. If you are behind the curve and don't use AI to help defend yourself, then you will find yourself on the back foot when it comes to preventing those attacks. Savvy partners have developed solutions that bake security directly into network equipment and which support hybrid environments to take security wherever your network goes. That includes built-in security capabilities like device profiling and NAC; centrally managed firewalls; and network-integrated AIOps that provide security teams with real-time information that can bolster security operations. Start by assessing your organization’s greatest source of risk, whether it’s expanding zero trust, supporting hybrid work, or defending against AI threats. There’s no right single place to begin but here are some options: Unified SASE provides secure access for users wherever they work, while simplifying both networking and security. Hybrid mesh firewalls offer consistent policies and enforcement across different network domains such as datacenters and cloud environments. Universal zero-trust network architectures support every identity, device, and workload. AIOps use AI to improve visibility, accelerate troubleshooting, and automate operations. These are different entry points into the same long-term architecture. Any and all of them will advance your journey to integrated native network security. Which you choose depends on the specific challenges of your business. Xiaomi’s new 18 Pro phones improve on Samsung’s privacy display

Xiaomi’s new 18 Pro phones improve on Samsung’s privacy display

The Verge | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
﻿An international launch is coming later this year. ﻿An international launch is coming later this year. Xiaomi’s new 18 Pro and 18 Pro Max launched in China today, and feature a more flexible take on the privacy display tech introduced this year on Samsung’s Galaxy S26 Ultra. They combine it with returning rear screens, two 200-megapixel cameras, and both of Qualcomm’s new Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 6 chips. A global launch is confirmed for later this year — especially welcome since last year’s 17 Pro phones skipped international markets. The two 18 Pro phones look similar to the 17 Pros, with similar compact displays built around the rear cameras, capable of displaying notifications, taking selfies, playing simple games, and more. The bigger changes are to the front screen, which is now equipped with hardware-level privacy features much like the S26 Ultra’s. Xiaomi’s implementation looks smarter than Samsung’s though: when you activate it for a specific app, it will not only turn on when that app is opened, but apply to notifications, floating windows, and split-screen views, without affecting the rest of the display. Location-based triggers allow you to set it to turn on when you reach work, or off when you come home, with smart triggers for when it detects locations like airports or elevators. It can also use the selfie camera to detect prying eyes and suggest activating the privacy mode. The two phones differ in size, with a 6.4-inch display on the Pro and 6.9 inches for the Pro Max. Battery varies accordingly: 7,000mAh or 8,500mAh respectively, using a high 32 percent silicon content. The other difference between the two is the chipset, with the Snapdragon 8 Gen 6 Elite in the Pro and the Extreme version in the Pro Max, though differences between the two chips are slight. Cameras are led by a 200-megapixel main camera using a large 1/1.28-inch-type sensor (the Light Fusion 960 in the Pro, and 960L in the Pro Max, though they seem similar). A 200-megapixel, 75mm telephoto joins it, the same on both phones, using Samsung’s 1/1.56-inch HP5 sensor and with a minimum focus distance of 12cm. A 50-megapixel ultrawide completes the trio. Xiaomi’s launch follows similar announcements this week for the Vivo X500 and Oppo Find X10 flagships. The two 18 Pro phones start from ¥5,999 (about $900), and join the 18 Fold in Xiaomi’s new flagship lineup, but we’re still waiting for the regular Xiaomi 18 and the 18 Ultra, likely to appear next spring. An international launch for at least one 18 Pro phone will come later this year, though we’re still waiting to hear if the Fold will ever leave China.
